The inventory knows these resource types (the values of `--type`); each platform syncs the ones it supports, listed with its commands below:

| Type | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `domain` | registered domains |
| `zone` | DNS zones |
| `dns_record` | DNS records |
| `instance` | compute instances |
| `security_group` | security groups and firewalls |
| `load_balancer` | load balancers |
| `certificate` | certificates held by a platform |
| `volume` | block storage volumes |
| `db_instance` | managed database instances |
| `eip` | elastic / static public IP addresses |
| `key_pair` | SSH key pairs |
| `r2_bucket` | Cloudflare R2 buckets (default jurisdiction) |

```text
polyctl sync [--platform cloudflare,aws] [--profile <name>] [--type domain,zone,dns_record,instance,...]
polyctl inventory stats
polyctl inventory list [--platform cloudflare,aws] [--type domain,zone,dns_record,instance,...] [--name <exact name>] [--profile <name>]
polyctl inventory path
```

- `sync` fetches the selected resource types (default: everything each platform supports) for the selected accounts (default: every account of every profile; `--profile` keeps one profile's accounts, `--platform` keeps the given platforms) and **replaces** them in the inventory atomically, one set at a time. It prints a per-set report (count, duration, failures). An authentication failure stops the sync of that account. The sets of `instance`, `load_balancer`, `eip` and `db_instance` on AWS and Alibaba Cloud, and `volume` on AWS, cover every enabled region of the account (GCP lists every region in one call); a region (or GCP zone) that fails is reported in the set's status while the regions that worked are stored and the failed ones keep what the inventory held for them, and the set counts as failed for the exit code. An unknown `--profile` is an error (exit code 4), an unknown platform or type a usage error (exit code 2). Having nothing to sync is not an error: when the selection names no account (a `--profile` without an account on the given `--platform`, or a platform nobody is logged in to), when no selected account supports the requested `--type`, or when no account is configured at all, `sync` prints an empty report on stdout (the table header, or `[]` with `-o json`) and a `nothing to do: ...` note on stderr, and exits with code 0.
- `inventory stats` shows row counts and the synced/expiry time of each set; `inventory list` dumps cached rows, filtered by `--profile`, `--name` and by `--platform` / `--type`, which take lists exactly as on `sync` (comma-separated or repeated; an unknown platform or type is a usage error); `inventory path` prints the database path.

## Platform-specific commands

Each platform has its own subtree, named after its platform id: `cloudflare` (also available under the alias `cf`), `namecom`, `godaddy`, `aws`, `gcp`, `aliyun`. Profile selection for these commands is `--profile` (which must have an account on that platform, otherwise the command fails with exit code 3) > `current_profile` (if it has an account on that platform) > the only profile that has one > an error listing the candidates (exit code 5). They honour `--refresh` / `--offline` for list operations the same way the neutral commands do, and always write through to the platform.

The trees follow each platform's own model, but the same operation has the same verb and shape in all of them:

- **`<platform> whoami`** verifies the account's credentials against the platform and shows the identity behind them. It exists at the top of all six trees and prints the same shape everywhere: `profile`, `platform` and `identity` (`account`, `user`, and an `extra` map with what else the platform reports). It needs the network (exit code 6 with `--offline`); credentials that no longer work exit with code 3.
- **Registrar groups** have `list`, `get <domain>`, `ns get <domain>` and `ns set <domain> <nameserver>...`.
- **Platforms that give every record an id** (Cloudflare, name.com, Alibaba Cloud) have `create`, `update <zone-or-domain> <record-id>` and `delete <zone-or-domain> <record-id>`. The record id is required there, so it is an argument.
- **Platforms that address records by name and type** (Route 53, Cloud DNS, GoDaddy) have `upsert`, which sets the whole record set, and a `delete` that takes `--name` / `--type`. There `--id` is an optional second way to pick one record, so it stays a flag, as on the neutral `dns delete`.
- **Compute services** name the resource: `<service> instance list|start|stop`.
- **`ensure`** converges a group of related resources to a desired state, where `create` makes one new resource (and fails when it exists) and `set` / `upsert` set one object (a DNS record, a record set) to the given value. An `ensure` command is idempotent: it first reads what exists, changes only what differs, reports `unchanged` for what already matches, and never deletes resources it was not told about. Any command that implicitly creates resources the caller did not name one by one (dependencies such as a VPC, a security group, a certificate, a listener) is an `ensure` command; `set` and `upsert` never create anything but their target. `ensure` waits until the resources are usable (`--no-wait` returns at once, `--timeout` bounds the wait) and prints a change report (see [Dry runs and change reports](#dry-runs-and-change-reports)). Exit code 0 includes "everything was unchanged".
- **Asynchronous changes** take `--wait` (wait until the change has completed; the default of `ensure` commands and of every `instance create`), `--no-wait` (return once the change was requested; accepted by every command that has `--wait`, a no-op where waiting is not the default) and `--timeout <duration>` (the upper bound of the wait, default `10m`). A wait that runs out of time fails with exit code 1 and names the resource and its last status.

### Cloudflare (`polyctl cloudflare`, alias `polyctl cf`)

```text
cloudflare whoami                 # verifies the API token (id, status, expiry) or the browser authorization (granted scopes)
cloudflare zone list              cloudflare zone get <zone|id>
cloudflare zone create <domain> [--foundation-dns[=false]]
cloudflare zone ensure <domain> [--preset optimized] [--ssl flexible|strict] [--foundation-dns[=false]]
cloudflare zone delete <zone|id> [-y] [--downgrade-enterprise]
cloudflare zone setting list <zone|id>
cloudflare zone setting get <zone|id> <setting>
cloudflare zone setting set <zone|id> <setting> <value> [--timeout 1m]
cloudflare dns list <zone> [--type] [--name]
cloudflare dns create <zone> --type A --name www --value 203.0.113.10 [--ttl 1] [--proxied[=false]] [--priority] [--comment]
cloudflare dns update <zone> <record-id> [--type] [--name] [--value ...] [--ttl ...] [--priority] [--proxied[=false]] [--comment]
cloudflare dns delete <zone> <record-id>
cloudflare cache purge <zone> (--everything | --url <url>... | --host <h>... | --tag <t>... | --prefix <p>...)   # host/tag/prefix need Enterprise
cloudflare cache rule list <zone> [--wide]
cloudflare cache rule set  <zone> --file rules.yaml [--replace] [--first] [-y]
cloudflare redirect rule list <zone> [--wide]
cloudflare redirect rule set  <zone> --file rules.yaml [--replace] [--first] [-y]
cloudflare dev-mode <zone> [on|off]       # Development Mode (bypass cache for 3 hours)
cloudflare ssl-mode <zone> [off|flexible|full|strict]
cloudflare account list
cloudflare r2 bucket list [--wide]
cloudflare r2 domain list   <bucket>
cloudflare r2 domain get    <bucket> <domain>
cloudflare r2 domain attach <bucket> <domain> [--zone <zone>] [--disabled] [--min-tls 1.0|1.1|1.2|1.3]
cloudflare r2 domain update <bucket> <domain> [--enabled[=false]] [--min-tls <v>]
cloudflare r2 domain detach <bucket> <domain> [-y]
cloudflare r2 domain ensure <bucket> <domain> [--zone <zone>] [--disabled] [--min-tls <v>]
```

Every command above can be written with `cf` in place of `cloudflare` (`polyctl cf zone list`). Inventory types: `zone`, `dns_record`, `r2_bucket`. Record ids are the Cloudflare record ids; `proxied` is the orange cloud, a boolean flag: `--proxied` switches it on, `--proxied=false` off, and without the flag `dns update` keeps the current setting.

`cloudflare zone create`, `ensure`, `delete` and `setting` manage the zones themselves:

- **`zone create <domain>`** adds the zone to the profile's account (the profile is chosen as for every Cloudflare command; the account is the one the profile is pinned to, or the only one its credentials see, and several are a usage error that asks you to pin one with `--set account_id`). `<domain>` is a registered domain such as `example.com`, lower-cased and without its trailing dot; a value that is plainly not one (no dot, a URL, an IP address, spaces) is a usage error (exit code 2) and the API judges the rest (exit code 1 with its message). The zone is created as a full zone and is `pending` until its nameservers are set at the registrar: polyctl does not wait for it to become active. Foundation DNS is then switched on for it (`--foundation-dns=false` skips that step); an account that cannot use it keeps the default nameservers and the step is reported `skipped`, while any other refusal fails the command (the zone stays created). The zone is read again afterwards, and its nameservers (the Foundation DNS ones when it was switched on) are printed below the report, `Nameservers (set these at your registrar):`, with the matching `polyctl domain ns set` command on stderr; polyctl never changes them at the registrar itself. A zone that exists in the account is exit code 1 (use `ensure`); so is a zone of that name in another account, which Cloudflare refuses (`already exists`, code 1061; pass `--profile` for that account).
- **`zone ensure <domain>`** reuses the zone when it exists in the account (`unchanged`) and creates it as `create` does when it is missing. With `--preset optimized` it then converges these settings, in this order, reading each first and writing only what differs: `0rtt` on, `early_hints` on, `rum` on, `response_buffering` on, `ipv6` off and Argo Smart Routing on (these three on Enterprise zones only, `skipped` with `enterprise only (plan=free)` elsewhere: the API accepts `ipv6` and Argo on other plans too, and Argo is billed there, so this is polyctl's policy), `always_use_https` on, `automatic_https_rewrites` on, and last `ssl`: `--ssl` (`flexible` or `strict`), else `defaults.cloudflare.ssl`, else `strict`. `strict` replaces `flexible` on a zone that has it (the report says `flexible -> strict`): pass `--ssl flexible` for an origin without HTTPS; `off` and `full` are set with `cloudflare ssl-mode`. `--ssl` without `--preset` sets the SSL mode only. A setting the zone's plan cannot change (`editable: false`, or refused with code 1015) is `skipped`. `--foundation-dns` matters for a new zone (on by default); an existing zone's Foundation DNS is left alone unless `--foundation-dns` is given, which switches it on (it is never switched off). ensure does not wait: a new zone stays `pending`, and there is no `--wait`. A failure stops the run where it is, without undoing anything; running it again goes on from there. Report rows: `zone/<domain>`, `zone/<domain>/foundation-dns`, `zone/<domain>/setting/<id>` (`updated` with `from -> to`, in JSON also `attrs.from` / `attrs.to`). JSON: `{"target": {"platform", "profile", "account_id"}, "dry_run", "zone": {"id", "name", "status", "plan", "name_servers", "original_name_servers"}, "changes"}`. `--dry-run` reads the zone and its settings and reports `would create` / `would update`; for a zone that does not exist yet its id and the current values are `(pending)` and the Enterprise-only settings are `skipped` (`plan unknown until created`). A failure (not a usage error, not a dry run) is sent to `hooks.on_failure` as `cloudflare.zone.ensure.failed` (`cloudflare.zone.create.failed` for `create`).
- **`zone delete <zone>`** deletes a zone (a name or a zone id) with all of its DNS records. The zone is found among the Cloudflare accounts, narrowed by `--profile` (a name in several accounts is exit code 5), and read live; the confirmation shows its plan, status and number of DNS records. An Enterprise zone must be moved to the Free plan first: polyctl does that only with `--downgrade-enterprise` (it changes the zone's billing) and otherwise stops with exit code 1, asking you to downgrade it in the dashboard; a token without billing permission is exit code 3 and nothing is deleted. When the downgrade succeeds and the deletion fails, the zone stays on the Free plan: run the command again. `--dry-run` reports `zone/<domain>  would delete  plan=free records=12` (and `zone/<domain>/subscription  would update  enterprise -> free`). The zone and its records leave the inventory.
- **`zone setting list <zone>`** lists the settings of a zone (`SETTING`, `VALUE`, `EDITABLE`, `MODIFIED_ON`; JSON `[{"id", "value", "editable", "modified_on", "zone"}]`); `rum`, `argo_smart_routing` and `foundation_dns` are not in that listing and are read with `get`. **`zone setting get <zone> <setting>`** shows one setting, by its Cloudflare id (`ssl`, `always_use_https`, `0rtt`, `min_tls_version`, ...) or one of the two names of separate endpoints: `argo_smart_routing` (on / off) and `foundation_dns` (true / false). **`zone setting set <zone> <setting> <value>`** reads the setting first and reports `unchanged` without writing when it already has the value, else `updated  from -> to`. Booleans take `on` / `off` (`true` / `false` are accepted and written as on / off); other values go to the API as given (as a number or a JSON object when the setting holds one), and the API judges them (exit code 1 with its message). polyctl does not keep a list of settings: an unknown one is not found (exit code 4), one the plan cannot change is exit code 7. Switching `foundation_dns` changes the zone's nameservers: `set` waits (up to `--timeout`, default 1m, polling every 5 seconds) until the zone reports the new ones and prints them with the `polyctl domain ns set` command to run. `--dry-run` reads and reports `would update`.
- These commands read the platform, so `--offline` is refused (exit code 6). A zone the inventory remembers but Cloudflare no longer has (deleted in the dashboard) is removed from the inventory with its records and looked up again by name; when that finds nothing it is not found (exit code 4). A zone the inventory does not know triggers one sync of the Cloudflare accounts. They need an API token with zone edit permission (creating a zone needs it at the account level) and zone settings edit permission; downgrading an Enterprise zone needs billing permission.

`cloudflare cache rule` and `cloudflare redirect rule` manage the cache rules (Cache Rules, phase `http_request_cache_settings`) and the redirect rules (Single Redirects, phase `http_request_dynamic_redirect`) of a zone. Cloudflare keeps the rules of a phase in one list (the zone's entrypoint ruleset) and replaces the whole list on every write, so polyctl always reads before it writes.

- **`rule list <zone>`** reads the rules live, in execution order (`#`, `ENABLED`, `DESCRIPTION`, `EXPRESSION`, and for cache rules `CACHE`, `EDGE_TTL`, `BROWSER_TTL`, for redirect rules `STATUS`, `TARGET`, `KEEP_QUERY`). The table cuts expressions at 40 characters; `--wide` prints them whole and adds `STATUS_TTL` (status code TTLs as `200-299:2678400, 400+:0`, `<=199:60`, `404:0`) and `REF`. TTLs read `override 2678400s (31d)`, `0s (no cache)`, `respect origin`, `bypass by default`, or `default` for a rule that caches without an edge TTL; a rule without a description shows `(no description)`. A zone that never had rules of the phase lists none (exit code 0). JSON: `{"zone": {"id", "name"}, "phase", "ruleset_id", "rules"}`, where each rule is cleaned to `description`, `ref` (only a reference of its own; Cloudflare sets it to the rule id otherwise), `enabled` (always written out), `action`, `expression` and `action_parameters` (with nulls dropped and `false` / `0` kept), with nothing cut: the `-o yaml` / `-o json` output is a valid `--file` for `set`. Rules are not kept in the inventory, so `--offline` is refused (exit code 6).
- **`rule set <zone> --file <rules.yaml>`** writes the rules of a YAML or JSON file (`-` reads stdin): a `rules:` list, or a bare list, each rule with the keys of the Cloudflare API, and only those: `description`, `action` (`set_cache_settings` for cache rules, `redirect` for redirect rules), `expression`, `enabled` (default `true`), `action_parameters` and optionally `ref`. `description`, `action` and `expression` are required, and descriptions must be unique within the file, since they identify the rules (exact match, case-sensitive). Any other key, a missing field, a duplicate description, a wrong action or an unreadable file is a usage error (exit code 2) naming the rule, before anything is sent. The parameters are passed to the API as written and the API judges them. By default the file is merged: a rule whose description matches an existing one replaces it in place, a new rule is appended at the end (`--first` puts the new rules first, in file order; mind a catch-all rule at the end, since the order is the execution order), and existing rules the file does not name (including rules without a description) are kept where they are, so nothing is ever deleted. When several existing rules share a description, the first is replaced and the others are kept with a warning. A file rule without `ref` keeps the `ref` of the rule it replaces. The merged list is compared with what exists, order included (a missing `enabled` counts as `true`): when it is the same, nothing is written and every rule of the file is reported `unchanged`; otherwise the whole list is written with one request, which succeeds or fails as a whole (a failure leaves the rules as they were; run the command again). `--replace` makes the file the whole list: existing rules it does not name are deleted (`deleted`), which asks for a confirmation (default no) in the interactive flow, after listing them, and needs `--yes` in the non-interactive one (exit code 2 without it); a declined confirmation writes nothing and exits with code 0. `--first` and `--replace` cannot be combined. The writes have no lock: run one `set` per zone and phase at a time.
- The change report has a row per rule of the file (`zone/<zone>/cache-rule/<description>` or `redirect-rule/...`): `unchanged`, `created` (`position 2`), `updated` with what changed (`edge_ttl.default 3600 -> 2678400`, `expression`, `enabled true -> false`, `position 3 -> 1`), `deleted` (`--replace`); kept rules that moved are reported `updated` too. A last row, `zone/<zone>/cache-rules  updated  PUT entrypoint (4 rules)` (`created` when the zone had no rules of the phase yet), stands for the one write. `--dry-run` reads the rules and reports `would create` / `would update` / `would delete` without writing or asking.
- Errors: a zone that no longer exists is not found (exit code 4) and leaves the inventory (Cloudflare answers HTTP 403 for it, so polyctl reads the zone to tell it from a missing permission); a token without the Rulesets permission of the phase is exit code 3; more rules than the zone's plan allows is exit code 7 (Free zones allow 10 cache rules); an invalid expression or parameter is exit code 1 with the API's message and the rule it is about.

`cloudflare r2` manages the custom domains of R2 buckets (the objects in a bucket, and creating or deleting buckets, are not covered). R2 is addressed per account, and buckets are not cached in the inventory: every `r2` command reads the platform, so `--offline` is refused (exit code 6) and `--refresh` changes nothing.

- **Accounts.** The account of a profile is the one it is pinned to (`account_id`), or the only account its credentials see; several are ambiguous (exit code 5). `r2 bucket list` without `--profile` lists the buckets of every Cloudflare profile (an account that has not enabled R2 is skipped silently, one that fails is a warning, and the command fails only when no account could be read); with `--profile`, an account without R2 is exit code 7. The buckets of the default jurisdiction are kept in the inventory as the resource type `r2_bucket` (fresh for an hour; `polyctl sync --type r2_bucket` fills it, `--refresh` reads them again, `--offline` lists only what the inventory holds; `--wide` and `--jurisdiction` read live and are exit code 6 with `--offline`). A sync of an account that cannot use R2 (R2 not enabled, no R2 permission, several accounts and none pinned) stores no bucket and prints a warning instead of failing the sync. The `r2 domain` commands without `--profile` first ask the inventory: when the fresh `r2_bucket` sets of every Cloudflare profile name exactly one account for `<bucket>`, that account is used (`bucket <name> via cloudflare/<profile> (local inventory)`) without listing every profile's buckets. Otherwise, and always with `--refresh` or `--jurisdiction`, they find the account that holds `<bucket>` among all Cloudflare profiles live: accounts without R2, or whose credentials are refused, are skipped (and named when the bucket is found nowhere, exit code 4), and a bucket name held by several accounts is ambiguous (exit code 5, pass `--profile`). `--jurisdiction <j>` (`eu`, `fedramp`) addresses the buckets of that R2 jurisdiction instead of the default one.
- **`bucket list`** shows each bucket's profile, name and creation date; `--wide` also reads each bucket (one request per bucket) for its location, storage class and jurisdiction. JSON: `[{"profile", "account_id", "name", "creation_date"}]`.
- **`domain list` / `get`** show each custom domain with its enabled state, zone, minimum TLS version (`-`, `null` in JSON, when it was never set: Cloudflare then applies its own default) and the ownership and SSL status Cloudflare reports (`pending` and `initializing` right after a binding are normal). A domain that is not bound to the bucket is not found (exit code 4).
- **`domain attach`** binds `<domain>` (lower-cased, without a trailing dot; wildcards are refused) to the bucket. The zone is `--zone` (a name or an id) or else the longest zone name the domain ends in (`cdn.example.co.uk` finds `example.co.uk`, a delegated `a.example.com` beats `example.com`), always among the zones of the bucket's account; it must be active, and the domain must be in it (a usage error otherwise). Cloudflare then creates the proxied CNAME record `<domain> -> public.r2.dev` in the zone; it is reported as `r2/<bucket>/domain/<domain>/dns-record` once it could be read, and stored in the inventory. A domain that is bound already (to this bucket or another) is an error (exit code 1), and so is an A, AAAA or CNAME record of that name in the zone, which Cloudflare would refuse (a TXT record is fine). `--disabled` creates the binding disabled; `--min-tls` sets the minimum TLS version (`1.0`, `1.1`, `1.2` or `1.3`; not set by default).
- **`domain update`** changes what is given (`--enabled` / `--enabled=false`, `--min-tls`; at least one is required) and leaves the rest. **`domain detach`** unbinds the domain, and Cloudflare deletes its CNAME record; it asks for confirmation (default no) in the interactive flow and needs `--yes` in the non-interactive one. Both read the domain first, because the API reports success for a domain that is not bound: such a domain is not found (exit code 4).
- **`domain ensure`** is the idempotent form: a binding that is enabled as wanted (disabled with `--disabled`) and, when `--min-tls` is given, with that version, is `unchanged` and nothing is written; a differing one is updated; a missing one is created as `attach` does. A binding with another zone than `--zone` is an error: it is never unbound automatically. A domain bound to another bucket is not looked for beforehand; the creation then fails.
- The writes print a change report and honour `--dry-run`; a browser login (`method=oauth`) does not cover R2, so these commands need an API token with R2 read and write permission (binding a domain also needs DNS edit permission on its zone).

### name.com (`polyctl namecom`)

```text
namecom whoami                    # GET /v4/hello: user name, environment, server time
namecom domain list               namecom domain get <domain>
namecom domain ns get <domain>    namecom domain ns set <domain> <nameserver>...
namecom domain lock <domain>      namecom domain unlock <domain>
namecom domain autorenew <domain> on|off
namecom domain check <name>...  # availability and price
namecom dns list <domain> [--type] [--name]
namecom dns create <domain> --type A --name www --value 203.0.113.10 [--ttl 300] [--priority]
namecom dns update <domain> <record-id> [--type] [--name] [--value] [--ttl] [--priority]
namecom dns delete <domain> <record-id>
```

Inventory types: `domain`, `zone`, `dns_record` (every name.com domain is also its own zone).

### GoDaddy (`polyctl godaddy`)

```text
godaddy whoami                    # verified by listing one domain: method, environment
godaddy domain list [--status ACTIVE,EXPIRED]   godaddy domain get <domain>
godaddy domain ns get <domain>    godaddy domain ns set <domain> <nameserver>...
godaddy domain lock <domain>      godaddy domain unlock <domain>
godaddy domain autorenew <domain> on|off
godaddy domain check <name>...  # availability and price
godaddy dns list <domain> [--type] [--name]
godaddy dns add <domain> --type A --name www --value 203.0.113.10 [--ttl 600] [--priority]             # one more record; the others stay
godaddy dns upsert <domain> --type A --name www --value <v> [--value <v>...] [--ttl] [--priority]   # sets the whole name+type set
godaddy dns delete <domain> (--type A --name www | --id <TYPE|name|value>)
```

Inventory types: `domain`, `zone`, `dns_record`. GoDaddy has no per-record ids; records are addressed by type + name. `upsert` creates the record set or replaces all of it (the API's "replace all records of this type and name"), like `record upsert` on Route 53 and Cloud DNS; `add` appends one record to the set. polyctl derives a stable synthetic id of the form `TYPE|name|value` (each part percent-encoded, e.g. `TXT|@|v=spf1%20include:_spf.example.com%20~all`) so the neutral `dns` commands and `godaddy dns delete --id` can target single records.

### AWS (`polyctl aws`)

```text
aws whoami                                        # sts GetCallerIdentity: account, ARN, user id
aws route53 zone list                             aws route53 zone get <zone-id|name>
aws route53 record list <zone> [--type] [--name]
aws route53 record upsert <zone> --type A --name www --value 203.0.113.10 [--value ...] [--ttl 300]
aws route53 record delete <zone> --type A --name www               # deletes the whole record set
aws route53domains list                           aws route53domains get <domain>
aws route53domains ns get <domain>                aws route53domains ns set <domain> <nameserver>...
aws ec2 instance list [--region <r>] [--state <s>[,<s>...]] [--type <t>[,<t>...]] [--name <substring|pattern>]
aws ec2 instance get <name|id> [--field <name>] [--include-terminated] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 instance create <name> [--type <type|size>] [--ami <ami-id> | --os al2023|windows] [--arch x86_64|arm64] [--subnet <id>] [--sg <sg-id>]...
                        [--key-pair <name>] [--ssh-key <public key> | --ssh-key-file <path>] [--disk <GB> [--disk-device /dev/sdb]
                        [--disk-type gp3] [--disk-iops 3000] [--disk-throughput 125]] [--public-ip[=false]] [--tag k=v]...
                        [--key-file <rsa private key>] [--password-timeout 20m]          # Windows only
                        [--region <r>] [--no-wait] [--timeout 5m]
aws ec2 instance password <name|id> [--key-file <path>] [--region <r>]                 # Windows user name and password
aws ec2 instance delete <name|id> [--yes] [--wait [--timeout 5m]] [--region <r>]       # alias: terminate
aws ec2 instance reboot <name|id> [--wait [--timeout 5m]] [--region <r>]               # starts a stopped instance
aws ec2 instance resize <name|id> --type <type|size> [--start[=false]] [--yes] [--timeout 5m] [--wait-port <n>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 instance start <name|id> [--region <r>] [--wait [--timeout 5m]]
aws ec2 instance stop <name|id> [--region <r>] [--force] [--yes] [--wait [--timeout 5m]]
aws ec2 key-pair list [--region <r>]
aws ec2 key-pair import <name> (--ssh-key <public key> | --ssh-key-file <path>) [--region <r>]
aws ec2 key-pair ensure [<name>] [--ssh-key <public key> | --ssh-key-file <path>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group list [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--name <name|pattern>] [--instance <name|id>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group get <sg-id|name> [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group create <name> [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--description <text>] [--rule <rule>]... [--rule-description <text>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group delete <sg-id|name> [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--yes] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group ensure <name> [--instance <name|id>] [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--rule <rule>]... [--description <text>]
                              [--rule-description <text>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group rule add <sg-id|name> --rule <rule>... [--rule-description <text>] [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 security-group rule remove <sg-id|name> --rule <rule>... [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--yes] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume list [--instance <name|id>] [--state available|in-use|creating|deleting|error] [--az <az>] [--name <glob>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume get <vol-id|name> [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume create [<name>] (--from-snapshot <snap-id> | --size <GB>) (--az <az> | --instance <name|id>) [--type gp3]
                      [--iops 3000] [--throughput 125] [--tag k=v]... [--wait [--timeout 5m]] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume resize [<vol-id|name>] [--size <GB>] [--iops <n>] [--throughput <n>] [--type <t>]
                      [--instance <name|id> [--device <dev>]] [--no-wait] [--wait-complete] [--timeout 10m] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume attach <vol-id|name> --instance <name|id> [--device /dev/sdb] [--no-wait] [--timeout 5m] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume detach <vol-id|name> [--force] [--yes] [--no-wait] [--timeout 5m] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 volume delete <vol-id|name> [--yes] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 snapshot list [--volume <vol-id|name>] [--state completed|pending|error] [--latest] [--name <glob>] [--region <r>]
aws ec2 snapshot create <vol-id|name> [--description <text>] [--tag k=v]... [--wait [--timeout 30m]] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 load-balancer list [--type network|application] [--name <pattern>] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 load-balancer get <name|arn|dns-name> [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 load-balancer create <name> --type network|application --subnet <id>... [--scheme internet-facing|internal] [--sg <sg-id>]...
                               [--ip-address-type ipv4|dualstack] [--region <r>] [--wait [--timeout 10m]]
aws elbv2 load-balancer update <name|arn> [--sg <sg-id>]... [--subnet <id>]... [--ip-address-type ipv4|dualstack]
                               [--deletion-protection[=false]] [--cross-zone[=false]] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 load-balancer delete <name|arn> [--cascade] [--yes] [--wait [--timeout 10m]] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 load-balancer ensure <name> --instance <name|id> [--type network] [--listener tcp:<port>]... [--skip-https]
                               [--target-type instance|ip] [--subnet <id>]... [--sg <sg-id>]... [--wait-healthy]
                               [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 target-group list [--lb <name|arn|dns-name>] [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 target-group get <name|arn> [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 target-group create <name> --protocol TCP|TLS|UDP|TCP_UDP|HTTP|HTTPS --port <n> [--vpc <vpc-id>] [--target-type instance|ip]
                              [--health-check-protocol TCP|HTTP|HTTPS] [--health-check-port traffic-port|<n>]
                              [--health-check-interval <s>] [--target <id[:port]>]... [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 target-group update <name|arn> [--add-target <id[:port]>]... [--remove-target <id[:port]>]... [--set-target <id[:port]>]...
                              [--health-check-protocol TCP|HTTP|HTTPS] [--health-check-interval <s>] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 target-group delete <name|arn> [--yes] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 listener list <lb> [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 listener get <listener-arn|lb:port> [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 listener create <lb> --protocol TCP|TLS|UDP|TCP_UDP|HTTP|HTTPS --port <n> --target-group <name|arn> [--cert <acm-arn>] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 listener update <listener-arn|lb:port> [--target-group <name|arn>] [--protocol <p>] [--region <r>]
aws elbv2 listener delete <listener-arn|lb:port> [--yes] [--region <r>]
aws acm certificate list [--key-type <t>]... [--status <s>]... [--domain <name>] [--region <r>|all]
aws acm certificate get <domain|arn> [--key-type <t>]... [--validation-records] [--region <r>]
aws acm certificate request <domain> [--wildcard[=false]] [--san <name>]... [--key-algorithm EC_prime256v1] [--tag k=v]...
                            [--no-wait] [--timeout 5m] [--region <r>]
aws acm certificate import <domain> [--cert-file <pem> --key-file <pem> [--chain-file <pem>]] [--arn <arn> | --new]
                           [--tag k=v]... [--staging] [--region <r>]
aws rds instance list [--state <s>] [--class <c>] [--name <glob>] [--region <r>|all]
aws rds instance get <id|endpoint-dns> [--region <r>]
aws rds instance create <id> --size <GB> --az <zone> (--password-stdin | --password-env <VAR>) [--engine mysql]
                        [--engine-version <v>|latest] [--major 8.0] [--class <db.xxx>] [--master-user admin] [--max-size 1000]
                        [--storage-type gp3] [--subnet-group <name>] [--security-group <sg-id>]... [--parameter-group <name>]
                        [--backup-retention 7] [--backup-window 03:00-04:00] [--maintenance-window sun:04:00-sun:05:00]
                        [--tag k=v]... [--public[=false]] [--wait[=false]] [--timeout 30m] [--region <r>]
aws rds instance start <id|endpoint-dns> [--wait [--timeout 30m]] [--region <r>]
aws rds instance stop <id|endpoint-dns> [--wait [--timeout 30m]] [--region <r>]
aws rds instance resize <id|endpoint-dns> --class <db.xxx> [--no-wait] [--timeout 60m] [--region <r>]
aws rds instance delete <id|endpoint-dns> [--final-snapshot <name> | --skip-final-snapshot] [--keep-protection] [--yes]
                        [--wait [--timeout 60m]] [--region <r>]
aws rds instance ensure <id|endpoint-dns>... --state stopped|running [--no-wait] [--timeout 30m] [--continue-on-error[=false]]
                        [--region <r>]
```

Inventory types: `zone`, `dns_record`, `domain`, `instance`, `key_pair`, `security_group`, `load_balancer`, `eip`, `db_instance`, `certificate`, `volume` (EBS volumes of every enabled region). `route53domains` is this tree's registrar group, the counterpart of `domain` in the other trees.

EC2 instances:

- `get`, `delete`, `reboot` and `resize` take the instance's Name tag or its id and find the account themselves, the way domains are routed: the inventory tells which AWS accounts hold an instance of that name or id (the stale instance lists are synced once when none does), and those accounts are asked live; when none of them holds a live one, the other selected accounts are asked too. Terminated instances, which AWS keeps listing for a while with their Name tag, are never picked. Several live instances of one name are ambiguous (exit code 5): pass `--profile`, or the instance id when they are in one account. Nothing found is exit code 4. The inventory holds the instances of every enabled region (so an instance in another region is found by name too); `list` without `--region` shows those of the profile's region, and `--region` asks the selected profile's account in another region directly.
- `list` filters ask AWS directly, bypassing the inventory: `--state` (`running`, `stopped`, `stopping`, `pending`, `terminated`, `shutting-down`) and `--type` take comma-separated values, `--name` matches Name tags that contain the value (a value holding `*` or `?` is used as the EC2 wildcard pattern it is). Several filters must all match. Without filters every state is listed, as before. No match is an empty list, a note on stderr and exit code 0.
- `get` shows the instance in any state but terminated (`--include-terminated` finds a terminated one too), with its addresses, network, key pair and volumes. In JSON: `{"instance_id", "instance_name", "profile", "region", "availability_zone", "instance_type", "state", "platform", "architecture", "private_ip", "public_ip", "vpc_id", "subnet_id", "security_groups", "key_name", "launch_time", "root_device", "volumes": [{"volume_id", "size", "device", "state", "volume_type", "delete_on_termination"}]}`. `--field <name>` prints the raw value of one of those scalar fields for scripts; an empty value (a stopped instance has no public IP) is exit code 1 with an explanation. A failed volume listing is a warning and an empty `volumes`.
- `create <name>` refuses a name that a non-terminated instance already has in the region (exit code 1, nothing written). `--type`, `--subnet`, `--sg` and `--key-pair` default to `defaults.aws.instance_type`, `subnet_id`, `security_group_ids` and `key_name`; a terminal asks for a missing name, subnet and type (see [Interactive and non-interactive use](#interactive-and-non-interactive-use)) and the non-interactive flow is a usage error (exit code 2), no security group means the VPC's default one. A key pair is required: Linux instances log in with a key only and polyctl never sets or enables a password, so without `--key-pair`, `--ssh-key`, `--ssh-key-file` or an existing `defaults.aws.key_name` a terminal offers the existing key pairs of the region or importing a new one (see [Missing input in the interactive flow](#missing-input-in-the-interactive-flow)) and the non-interactive flow is exit code 2 before anything is read or created. The image is `--ami`, or the latest Amazon Linux 2023 image for the type's architecture (`--arch`; x86_64 by default, arm64 only for a type that runs nothing else, such as `t4g.small`): the SSM public parameter `al2023-ami-kernel-default-<arch>` first, and, when it cannot be read (no `ssm:GetParameter` permission) or its image is not available, the newest standard Amazon image named `al2023-ami-2023.*-kernel-*-<arch>` of any kernel line (highest release, then latest creation time; minimal images excluded), so the two can use different kernels; a `<cores>H<memory>G` size resolves to an x86_64 type, and polyctl never picks arm64 by itself. An image polyctl picks is always available and not deprecated (`--ami` is used as given). No such image is exit code 4; `--ami` takes any other image. Mac types (`x86_64_mac`, `arm64_mac`) have no default image and need `--ami` (exit code 7 without it). `--os windows` takes the newest Windows Server English Full Base image (x86_64): the highest year among the public SSM parameters `/aws/service/ami-windows-latest/Windows_Server-<year>-English-Full-Base` (`ssm:GetParametersByPath`, so a new Windows Server release is picked up without a polyctl update), and, when they cannot be read or that image is not available, the newest available, not deprecated Amazon image named `Windows_Server-<year>-English-Full-Base-<date>` (highest year, then latest date); an arm64 type or `--arch arm64` is exit code 2, a Mac type exit code 7. A Windows instance launches with an RSA key pair and its login is stored and shown as described in [Instance login credentials](#instance-login-credentials): `--ssh-key`, `--ssh-key-file`, and `--key-pair` without `--key-file` are exit code 2 for it, `defaults.aws.key_name` is not used, and `--key-file` is exit code 2 for a Linux instance. The network is one interface in the subnet with the security groups and, unless `--public-ip=false`, a public IP. `--ssh-key` / `--ssh-key-file` bind the key pair that holds that public key, importing it under `--key-pair` when none does (the rules of `key-pair ensure`). `--disk <GB>` adds a data disk (`/dev/sdb`, gp3 with 3000 IOPS and 125 MiB/s by default; IOPS and throughput are sent for gp3 only, io1 and io2 need `--disk-iops`) that is **kept** when the instance is deleted; its encryption follows the account's EBS default. The Name tag, the tags of `defaults.aws.tags` and `--tag k=v` go on the instance and its volumes. create waits until the instance runs (by default; 5 second polls, `--no-wait` returns at once) and reports its public and private IP. `--dry-run` resolves the image and checks the name and the type, then reports `would create` with the type, image, subnet, security groups and disk.
- `delete` (alias `terminate`) shows the instance, its addresses and its volumes, says which volumes go with it (the root volume) and which stay (data volumes, which keep costing money), and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2). A declined confirmation is exit code 0. Termination protection is exit code 1 with the command that switches it off; polyctl never switches it off. A terminated instance is `unchanged`. Elastic IPs and snapshots are not deleted. `--wait` waits until it is terminated. Once the termination is requested, the stored Windows login of the instance is removed and the key pair polyctl created for it is deleted (a `key-pair/<name> deleted` row; a failure is a warning).
- `reboot` reboots a running instance and starts a stopped one; any other state is exit code 1. A reboot keeps the instance running, so `--wait` only applies to the start of a stopped instance (which gets a new public IP unless it has an Elastic IP).
- `resize --type <type|size>` checks the new type first (unknown: exit code 2; another architecture, x86_64 against arm64, or not offered in the instance's availability zone: exit code 1, nothing written), then stops a running instance (after a confirmation, as for `delete`), changes the type and starts it again, waiting for each state (5 second polls, `--timeout` each). The instance ends in the power state it had: a stopped instance stays stopped unless `--start`, and `--start=false` leaves a running one stopped. The same type is `unchanged`. A failed type change leaves the instance stopped with its old type; running the command again continues from there. Without an Elastic IP the public IP changes: the report and stderr show `public ip <old> -> <new>`. `--wait-port <n>` then waits until the TCP port accepts connections (22 for SSH, 3389 for RDP). The report has one row per step (`stop`, `modify-type <old> -> <new>`, `start`). A size (`--type 2H8G`) is resolved before anything changes: an instance whose type already has that many vCPUs and that memory is `unchanged`; otherwise the size is resolved in the instance's availability zone like for `create` (see instance sizes below). A size always resolves to an x86_64 type, so an arm64 instance needs an exact type.
- **Instance sizes.** `--type` of `create` and `resize` (and `defaults.aws.instance_type`) also takes a size `<cores>H<memory>G`: `2H2G`, `2H4G`, `4H8G`, `2H0.5G` (any case). Anything else is an instance type, used as it is. A size is resolved on every run, in a `--dry-run` too, and never remembered, because offerings change; it is resolved in the availability zone of the subnet (`create`) or of the instance (`resize`), or in the region when the instance has no zone. A burstable T3 type of exactly that shape comes first: `t3.nano` (2H0.5G), `t3.micro` (2H1G), `t3.small` (2H2G), `t3.medium` (2H4G), `t3.large` (2H8G), `t3.xlarge` (4H16G), `t3.2xlarge` (8H32G), when `DescribeInstanceTypeOfferings` lists it for that zone. Otherwise the candidates are the current-generation x86_64 types with exactly that many default vCPUs and MiB of memory (`DescribeInstanceTypes` filtered on `vcpu-info.default-vcpus`, `memory-info.size-in-mib`, `processor-info.supported-architecture=x86_64` and `current-generation=true`, every page) that the zone offers (`DescribeInstanceTypeOfferings`, 50 types a call), and the first by family wins: `t3`, `t3a`, `m7i`, `m6i`, `c7i`, `c6i`, `r7i`, `r6i`, `m5`, `c5`, `r5`, then every other family, alphabetically by type name within a rank. Prices are not compared. The choice is printed on stderr (`2H4G -> t3.medium (ap-east-1a, T3 preset)`, `4H8G -> c7i.xlarge (ap-east-1a, family preference)`) and the change report shows the type with the size (`type=t3.medium(2H4G)`; `-o json` adds `"attrs": {"instance_type", "size"}`). The memory must match exactly. No fit is exit code 4, naming the size and the zone (and the types of that size the zone does not offer); a size of 0 vCPUs or 0 GiB, or a size with `--arch arm64` (sizes resolve to x86_64 types), is exit code 2.
- `start` and `stop` find the instance like `get`. One already `running` (`start`) or `stopped` (`stop`) is `unchanged` and nothing is sent. `stop` asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`: exit code 2, before any lookup; a declined confirmation is exit code 0); `--force` stops without a clean shutdown. `--wait` waits until the instance is running (and prints its addresses) or stopped. The output is a change report: `instance/<name>  updated  start; running, public ip <ip>` (the IPs are in the JSON `attrs`), or `stop; stopped`; without `--wait` the detail is just `start` / `stop`.
- Errors: denied calls are exit code 3 and name the IAM action (`ec2:RunInstances`, ...); missing capacity (`InsufficientInstanceCapacity`) and exhausted quotas (`VcpuLimitExceeded`, `InstanceLimitExceeded`) are exit code 1 with what to do; a wait that runs out of time is exit code 1 with the last state.

EC2 key pairs belong to one region: `--region` picks it, the profile's region by default (the region the inventory caches; `key-pair list --region` reads another one live). AWS imports RSA and ED25519 public keys only; any other algorithm (ECDSA, DSA) is refused before anything is sent (exit code 2).

- `--ssh-key` takes the public key itself (one line, `ssh-ed25519 AAAA... comment`); `--ssh-key-file` a file holding it (`~` is expanded, the first public key line is used). A file path given to `--ssh-key`, both flags together, a private key, or text that is not a public key are usage errors (exit code 2). The public key is never printed: reports show its algorithm, the start of its SHA256 fingerprint and the first 20 characters of its comment.
- `key-pair list` shows `NAME`, `TYPE`, `FINGERPRINT` and `CREATED`. In JSON each key pair is `{"name", "key_pair_id", "type", "fingerprint", "created", "region"}` and `fingerprint` is exactly what AWS reports: unprefixed padded base64 SHA-256 for ED25519, colon-separated hexadecimal for RSA (the MD5 of the key for an imported key, a SHA-1 of the private key for a key pair AWS generated). The table prefixes ED25519 fingerprints with `SHA256:`. No key pairs is an empty list (`[]` with `-o json`), a note on stderr and exit code 0.
- `key-pair import <name>` imports the key under `<name>` and prints a change report (`key-pair/<name>  created`). A name that is taken is a usage error (exit code 2) whatever key it holds: an existing key pair is never replaced. `--dry-run` checks the key and reports `would create` without sending any request. The tags of `defaults.aws.tags` are set on the new key pair.
- `key-pair ensure [<name>]` reuses an existing key pair first. With a public key, the region's key pairs are compared with it by their public key (which matches imported key pairs and the ones AWS generated alike; a key pair listed without its public key is compared by fingerprint), and a key pair that holds it is reported `unchanged` (`matched by public key`) under its own name; a `<name>` that does not exist is then ignored, with a note. The same key can be imported under several names: `<name>` picks one of them, otherwise a terminal asks and the non-interactive flow fails with exit code 5, listing the names. When no key pair holds the key, it is imported as `<name>`; without `<name>` a terminal asks for the name, with no default, checked as it is typed (leaving the question creates nothing, exit code 0) and the non-interactive flow is a usage error (exit code 2): no name is ever chosen for you. With `<name>` alone, an existing key pair is used as it is; a missing one needs a public key (a terminal asks for the file, offering `~/.ssh/*.pub`; otherwise exit code 2). A `<name>` that exists with another key, or that AWS lists without its public key so it cannot be compared, is a usage error (exit code 2) and is never replaced. Without a key and without `<name>`, a terminal lists the existing key pairs (the one holding a `~/.ssh/*.pub` key marked) and "Import a new key pair from a public key file..."; the non-interactive flow is exit code 2. `--dry-run` reports `unchanged` or `would create` without writing.
- There is no `key-pair delete`: a key pair may still be bound to instances, and polyctl does not remove it.

EC2 security groups belong to one region and one VPC; names are unique within a VPC only.

- A group is named by its id (`sg-...`) or its name. A name is looked up in `--vpc`, else in `defaults.aws.vpc_id`, else in every VPC of the region, where a name that several VPCs use is ambiguous (exit code 5: pass `--vpc` or the id). A missing group is exit code 4, a malformed id (`sg-bogus`) exit code 2. Without `--profile` and `--region`, an id is looked up in the account whose inventory holds it when exactly one does.
- Rules are `<protocol>:<port|from-to|alias|all>:<source>`, the format every platform's firewall commands share: `tcp`, `udp`, `icmp` or `all`; a port, a range (`8000-8999`, aliases allowed on both ends: `ssh-http`) or an alias (`ssh`, `telnet`, `smtp`, `dns`, `http`, `pop3`, `imap`, `https`, `smb`, `mssql`, `mysql`, `rdp`, `postgresql`/`postgres`, `redis`, `elasticsearch`/`elastic`, `mongodb`/`mongo`); `icmp:all`, `all:all`, and `tcp:all` / `udp:all` for ports 1-65535; the source is an IPv4 or IPv6 CIDR (host bits are masked, a bare address gets `/32` or `/128`) or a security group id of the same account. ICMP from an IPv6 source is ICMPv6 (protocol 58). A malformed rule is exit code 2. Repeated rules in one command count once.
- Rules are compared by protocol, ports and source, never by description, the way AWS compares them. Egress rules are not managed: a new group keeps the AWS default that allows all outbound traffic.
- `list` shows `ID`, `NAME`, `VPC`, `RULES` (ingress rules, one per source) and `DESCRIPTION`; without filters the profile's region comes from the local inventory, while `--region`, `--vpc`, `--name` (`*` is a wildcard) and `--instance` (the groups attached to an instance, found like `instance get` finds it) ask AWS. No groups is `[]`, a note on stderr and exit code 0.
- `get` shows the group, its ingress rules and the instances it is attached to. In JSON: `{"id", "name", "vpc_id", "description", "profile", "region", "tags", "ingress": [{"protocol", "from_port", "to_port", "source", "source_type", "description"}], "instances"}`; `protocol` is `tcp`, `udp`, `icmp`, `icmpv6` or `all`, ports are -1 when the protocol has none, and `source_type` is `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `sg` or `prefix-list`.
- `create <name>` creates the group in `--vpc` (default `defaults.aws.vpc_id`; neither is exit code 2) with the description `auto created <name>` unless `--description` (AWS cannot change it later), then adds the `--rule` rules in one call, each with `--rule-description`. A group of that name in the VPC is exit code 1 (use `ensure`). Names may not start with `sg-`; names and descriptions take ASCII letters, digits, spaces and `._-:/()#,@[]+=&;{}!$*`, at most 255 characters (anything else is exit code 2 before anything is sent). New groups are tagged `Name=<name>`, `polyctl:managed=true` and the tags of `defaults.aws.tags` (which may override `polyctl:managed`).
- `ensure <name>` makes sure the group exists, holds the rules and, with `--instance`, is attached to that instance; a second run changes nothing. The VPC is `--vpc`, else the instance's VPC, else `defaults.aws.vpc_id` (none is exit code 2; a `--vpc` other than the instance's is exit code 2). A missing group is created as by `create`; an existing one is used as it is (`unchanged`). Rules present are `unchanged`; the missing ones are added in one call (AWS refuses a whole batch that holds a rule present already, so when another writer added one meanwhile, the rules are added one by one and the duplicate is `unchanged`). Rules are never removed. The instance (Name tag or id, found in every selected account like `instance get`) keeps every group it has: the new group is added to the set (`attached sg-... (kept N existing)`), and an attached group is `unchanged`. An instance that already has 5 groups (the default quota per network interface) is exit code 1, and one with several network interfaces exit code 7, before anything is written. `--dry-run` reports `would create` (the new group's id is `(pending)`, and all its rules would be created) and `instance/<name>  would update  attach <sg-id|(pending)> (keep: ...)`. Report rows: `security-group/<name>`, `security-group/<name>/rule` (detail `tcp 443 0.0.0.0/0`), `instance/<name>`.
- `rule add <sg-id|name>` adds the rules to an existing group (exit code 4 when it is missing), like `ensure` without creating or attaching anything. `rule remove` removes the rules that are present (a rule that is not there is `unchanged`) after showing them and asking for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2); nothing to remove asks nothing.
- `delete` shows the group's name, VPC and instances and asks for a confirmation (as for `rule remove`). A group still attached to an instance or network interface, or used as the source of another group's rule, is exit code 1 (`DependencyViolation`: detach it or remove the reference first). The default group of a VPC is refused (exit code 1). A group that is gone by the time it is deleted is `unchanged`.
- Errors: denied calls are exit code 3 and name the IAM action (`ec2:AuthorizeSecurityGroupIngress`, ...); quotas (`RulesPerSecurityGroupLimitExceeded`: 60 ingress rules per group by default, IPv4 and IPv6 counted apart; `SecurityGroupsPerInstanceLimitExceeded`) are exit code 1 with the AWS message.


EBS volumes (`volume`) and snapshots (`snapshot`). The inventory (`volume`) holds the volumes of every enabled region of each account; snapshots are not cached.

- A volume is named by its id (`vol-...`) or its Name tag. An id is looked up in the inventory of every selected account (one sync of their volumes when none holds it; the id does not tell the region), then read live; `--region` asks the selected profile's account in that region directly. A Name tag is looked up live in the region of each selected account (the profile's region, or `--region`). Several volumes of one name (in one account or across accounts) are ambiguous (exit code 5): pass `--profile`, or the volume id. Nothing found is exit code 4.
- `list` reads the volumes of the profile's region live (`--region` another one) and stores them in the inventory. `--instance` keeps the volumes attached to that instance (found like `instance get` finds it, in its own account and region), `--state` those in that state (`available` lists the volumes attached to nothing, the orphans a deleted instance can leave behind), `--az` those of that zone, `--name` those whose Name tag matches the value (`*` and `?` are wildcards). The table shows `ID`, `NAME`, `SIZE`, `TYPE`, `IOPS`, `THROUGHPUT`, `STATE`, `AZ`, `INSTANCE` (its name, or id) and `DEVICE`; in JSON each row is `{"volume_id", "name", "profile", "region", "availability_zone", "size", "volume_type", "iops", "throughput", "state", "instance_id", "instance_name", "device"}`. No match is an empty list, a note on stderr and exit code 0. With `--offline` the rows come from the inventory (`--instance` then needs AWS: exit code 6).
- `get` shows a volume. In JSON: `{"volume_id", "name", "profile", "region", "availability_zone", "size", "volume_type", "iops", "throughput", "state", "encrypted", "snapshot_id", "attachments": [{"instance_id", "instance_name", "device", "state", "delete_on_termination"}], "modification": {"state", "progress", "original_size", "target_size", "original_volume_type", "target_volume_type", "start_time"}}`. `modification` is the latest modification of the volume (AWS keeps only the latest one, for a limited time) and is left out when AWS keeps none.
- `create [<name>]` creates a volume: empty with `--size <GB>`, or from `--from-snapshot <snap-id>`, as large as the snapshot unless `--size` asks for more (a smaller `--size` is exit code 2: AWS would silently use the snapshot's size). The zone is `--az`, or the zone of `--instance`, whose account and region are used (a volume attaches only to instances of its zone); one of them is required, both are exit code 2. Without `--instance` the volume goes to the selected profile's account in the region of `--az` (the zone without its last letter; `--region` for other zones). The type is `gp3` with 3000 IOPS and 125 MiB/s by default; IOPS and throughput are sent for `gp3` only, `io1` and `io2` need `--iops`, `gp2`, `st1` and `sc1` take neither (exit code 2). Encryption follows the snapshot or the account's EBS default. The name is the Name tag (without it the volume has none); a volume of that name that is not deleted in the region is exit code 1. The tags of `defaults.aws.tags` and `--tag k=v` go on the volume (`--tag Name=...` sets the name). `--wait` waits until the volume is available (5 second polls; the `error` state fails). `--dry-run` checks the snapshot and the name, then reports `would create` with `size=... type=... az=... from snapshot ...` and the id `(pending)`.
- `resize` changes a volume in AWS: `--size` grows it (EBS volumes cannot shrink: a smaller size is exit code 2), `--type`, `--iops` and `--throughput` change its performance; only the fields given are sent (`--iops 0` included). The volume is named by its id or Name tag, or by `--instance` and `--device` (default: the instance's root device as the instance reports it, `/dev/xvda` on Amazon Linux): when no volume is attached as that device, the instance's only volume is taken if `--device` was not given, and otherwise the volumes are listed and the command stops (exit code 2) rather than guess. When every field given is as asked already the volume is `unchanged` and nothing is sent (AWS accepts such a request and counts it against the modification rate limit). `--type io1|io2` needs `--iops` unless the volume is provisioned-IOPS already (exit code 2); AWS checks the IOPS and throughput ranges (exit code 2 with its message; note that AWS silently keeps the old value for some out-of-range gp3 IOPS). A modification that is still `modifying` or `optimizing` is exit code 1 with its progress, also with `--dry-run`. The modification rate limit (`VolumeModificationRateExceeded`) is exit code 1 with the time AWS names for the next modification (AWS documents six hours between modifications; the time in its message is what counts). `resize` waits until the modification is `optimizing` or `completed`, when the new size is visible inside the instance (5 second polls, `--timeout 10m`; `--no-wait` returns at once); `--wait-complete` waits for `completed`, which can take hours for large volumes (`--timeout` then defaults to 6h); `failed` fails. The report reads `volume/vol-0222  updated  size 300 -> 400 (optimizing)`. polyctl does not grow the partition or file system; after a size change it prints the commands to run inside the instance (`sudo resize2fs /dev/nvme1n1` for a data disk holding the file system directly, `sudo growpart /dev/nvme0n1 1 && sudo xfs_growfs /` for an xfs root disk). `--dry-run` reports `would update` with `size 300 -> 500, iops 3000 -> 4000`.
- `attach <vol> --instance <name|id>` attaches the volume as `--device` (default `/dev/sdb`). A volume name is looked up in the instance's region (unless `--region`). The volume and the instance must be in one account and region (exit code 1) and one availability zone (exit code 1, checked before AWS is asked). A volume being created is waited for until it is available first. A volume attached to that instance as that device is `unchanged` (AWS itself refuses a repeated attachment); one attached elsewhere, or to that instance as another device, is exit code 1 (detach it first). A device the instance uses already is exit code 2. `attach` waits until the attachment is `attached`, not only until the volume reads `in-use` (5 second polls, `--timeout 5m`; `--no-wait` returns at once). Inside the instance the volume shows up under its NVMe name (`/dev/sdb` is `/dev/nvme1n1` or the like on Nitro instances): mounting it is up to you; a volume restored from a snapshot reads slowly at first, as AWS loads its blocks on first access. With `--dry-run` a volume name that does not exist yet (one an earlier step of the same dry run would create) is reported as `would update` with the id `(pending)`.
- `detach <vol>` shows the volume and its instance, reminds you to unmount it inside the instance first, and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2; a declined confirmation is exit code 0). A volume attached to nothing is `unchanged`, as is one being detached already (nothing is asked or sent; `detach` waits for it unless `--no-wait`, and `--force --yes` forces a detach that is stuck). The root volume of an instance is exit code 1 (stop or terminate the instance instead). `--force` detaches forcibly and can lose data, for a detach that is stuck; it always needs `--yes` (exit code 2). A volume still in use inside the instance (`VolumeInUse`) is exit code 1 with that advice. `detach` waits until the volume is available (5 second polls, `--timeout 5m`; `--no-wait` returns at once).
- `delete <vol>` shows the volume (name, size, type, zone, source snapshot) and its latest completed snapshot, or warns that it has none, and asks for a confirmation as `detach` does. An attached volume is exit code 1: detach it first; the data volumes of an instance being terminated stay in use until it is terminated, so wait for that (`instance delete --wait`). A volume being deleted, or one the inventory knew that is gone, is `unchanged`. Snapshots are kept. A recipe to read a snapshot on a temporary instance: `instance create tmp --wait`, `volume create data --from-snapshot snap-... --instance tmp --wait`, `volume attach data --instance tmp`, then after the work `instance delete tmp --yes --wait` and `volume delete data --yes`.
- `snapshot list` lists the snapshots the account owns (`OwnerIds self`: public snapshots of other accounts are never scanned) in the profile's region (`--region` another one), newest first. `--volume` keeps the snapshots of that volume (its id or name, found like `volume get` finds it, in its account and region; the id of a deleted volume still lists its snapshots, in the account and region the inventory last saw it in, else the selected profile's region), `--state` those in that state, `--name` those whose Name tag matches the value (`*` and `?` are wildcards), and `--latest` only the newest completed one. The table shows `ID`, `VOLUME`, `SIZE`, `STATE`, `PROGRESS`, `STARTED`, `DESCRIPTION` and `NAME`; in JSON each row is `{"id", "volume_id", "size", "state", "progress", "start_time", "description", "name", "encrypted", "profile", "region"}` (`progress` as AWS has it, `100%` when completed, empty right after the start). No match is an empty list, a note on stderr and exit code 0. Snapshots are not cached: `--offline` is exit code 6.
- `snapshot create <vol>` snapshots a volume. The Name tag is the volume's name (or id) and the UTC time, `<name>-20260102T0304Z`, unless `--tag Name=...`; the tags of `defaults.aws.tags` and `--tag k=v` go on it, `--description` sets the description. A snapshot is crash-consistent: stop the writes or unmount the file system first for an application-consistent copy (a note says so). A snapshot of the volume that is still pending is exit code 1 (`SnapshotCreationPerVolumeRateExceeded`: AWS takes one at a time per volume). `--wait` waits until it is completed (15 second polls, `--timeout 30m`; large volumes can take long; the `error` state fails). The report reads `snapshot/<name>  created  snapshot of vol-...` with the snapshot id; `--dry-run` reports `would create` with the id `(pending)`. Deleting, copying and multi-volume snapshots are not covered.
- Errors: denied calls are exit code 3 and name the IAM action (`ec2:CreateVolume`, ...); a missing volume, snapshot or instance is exit code 4; parameter errors AWS reports (`InvalidParameterValue`, `InvalidParameterCombination`) are exit code 2 with the AWS message; an exhausted volume quota (`VolumeLimitExceeded`), a zone mismatch (`InvalidVolume.ZoneMismatch`), a volume in use (`VolumeInUse`) and a refused state change (`IncorrectState`) are exit code 1 with the AWS message.

Elastic Load Balancing v2 (`elbv2`) covers Network and Application Load Balancers, their target groups and listeners. `load-balancer ensure` puts a Network Load Balancer in front of an instance in one command.

- A load balancer is named by its name, its ARN or its DNS name (compared without case and trailing dot). Names are unique within an account and region only. Without `--region` the inventory (`load_balancer`, every enabled region, with the DNS names) tells which account and region hold it; when it does not know, the stale load balancer sets are synced once, and then every selected account is asked live in its region (or the region a DNS name holds); an account that cannot be asked is a warning. Several matches are ambiguous (exit code 5: pass `--profile`, or `--region` / the ARN when one account holds the name in several regions); none is exit code 4. An ARN is looked up in its own region (another `--region` is exit code 2), in the account whose inventory holds load balancers of the ARN's account id. Target groups and listeners are looked up in the profile's region (`--region`, or the region of an ARN); a listener is its ARN or `<lb>:<port>`.
- `load-balancer list` shows `NAME`, `TYPE`, `SCHEME`, `STATE`, `VPC` and `DNS` of the profile's region from the local inventory; `--region` asks AWS about another region. `--type` and `--name` (`*` and `?` are wildcards) filter. JSON: `[{"name", "arn", "type", "scheme", "state", "vpc_id", "dns_name", "region"}]`; none is `[]`, a note on stderr and exit code 0.
- `load-balancer get` shows the load balancer, its zones, security groups, deletion protection and cross-zone attributes, its listeners and the target groups attached to it with the health of every target (`healthy`, `unhealthy`, `initial`, `draining`, `unused`, `unavailable`, with the reason). JSON: `{"arn", "name", "type", "scheme", "state", "vpc_id", "dns_name", "ip_address_type", "zones": [{"zone", "subnet_id"}], "security_groups", "region", "profile", "deletion_protection", "cross_zone", "listeners": [{"arn", "port", "protocol", "default_action", "target_groups", "certificates", "load_balancer"}], "target_groups": [{"arn", "name", "protocol", "port", "vpc_id", "target_type", "load_balancers", "health_check", "region", "profile", "targets": [{"id", "port", "zone", "state", "reason", "description"}]}]}`.
- `load-balancer create <name>` needs `--type` and at least one `--subnet` (one per availability zone); `--scheme` is `internet-facing` by default, `--ip-address-type` `ipv4`. Without `--sg` the request carries no security groups, and **a Network Load Balancer created without security groups can never get any** (AWS refuses `SetSecurityGroups` on it). A load balancer of that name in the region is exit code 1 (use `ensure`). New load balancers and target groups are tagged `polyctl:managed=true` and with the tags of `defaults.aws.tags`. `--wait` waits until it is active (15 second polls; about two to three minutes).
- `load-balancer update` changes only what its flags name, and only when it differs (otherwise `unchanged`): `--sg` replaces the security groups (on a Network Load Balancer created without any: exit code 1 before anything is sent; the list cannot be emptied), `--subnet` the subnets, `--ip-address-type` sets `ipv4` or `dualstack` (which needs subnets with an IPv6 block: AWS refuses it otherwise, exit code 1), `--deletion-protection[=false]` and `--cross-zone[=false]` the attributes. No flag is exit code 2.
- `load-balancer delete` shows the type, the DNS name, the number of listeners and the target groups, and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2; a declined confirmation is exit code 0). The listeners go with the load balancer; the target groups stay unless `--cascade`, which deletes them afterwards, retrying every 5 seconds for up to a minute while AWS still reports one in use; a group another load balancer still forwards to is `skipped` with a warning (at once when AWS lists that other load balancer on the group, also in a dry run). Deletion protection is exit code 1 with the `update --deletion-protection=false` command to run; polyctl never switches it off. `--wait` waits until the load balancer is no longer listed.
- `load-balancer ensure <name> --instance <name|id>` makes sure that, for every `--listener tcp:<port>` (default `tcp:80` and `tcp:443`; `--skip-https` is `tcp:80` alone and cannot be combined with `--listener`), a TCP target group on that port holds the instance, and that a Network Load Balancer has a TCP listener on that port forwarding to it. A second run changes nothing (`all unchanged` on stderr, no write call).
  - Names: the load balancer is `naming.aws_elbv2_load_balancer` (`{name}-lb`), the target group of a port `naming.aws_elbv2_target_group` (`{name}-{port}-target`; it must use `{port}` when there are several ports). The names are the key that finds the resources again: changing a template makes `ensure` build a new set. Every name is checked before anything is sent: at most 32 letters, digits and hyphens, no hyphen first or last, and a load balancer name may not start with `internal-` (exit code 2). With the default templates `<name>` can have at most 21 characters.
  - The instance (Name tag or id, found in every selected account like `instance get`) must be running (exit code 1 otherwise). The target groups are created in its VPC (TCP on the port, a TCP health check on the traffic port, the AWS default interval) with the instance id registered on the port, or its private address with `--target-type ip`. A target registered on another port, or draining, does not count and is registered again. A target group of the name with another protocol, port, VPC or target type is exit code 1 before anything is written, with the differences: those settings cannot change.
  - A missing load balancer is created internet-facing, `ipv4`, in the instance's subnet or the `--subnet` subnets (which must be in the instance's VPC and include its availability zone: AWS would accept the target and report it `unused` forever, so that is exit code 1), with the `--sg` security groups or `defaults.aws.elb_security_group_ids`. Without any, a note says it is created without security groups for good. It is waited for until active (`--no-wait` returns at once, `--timeout` bounds the wait; a load balancer that becomes `failed` is exit code 1). An existing load balancer of the name must be a network one in the instance's VPC and enabled in its availability zone (exit code 1 otherwise); its scheme and subnets are left alone, and security groups it cannot take, or other than those asked for, are reported `skipped`. A provisioning one is waited for.
  - A listener on the port that forwards elsewhere, or with another protocol, is modified in place (`updated`, detail `tcp:80 -> old-target -> tcp:80 -> site-80-target`), never deleted and recreated. A resource another writer creates meanwhile (`DuplicateTargetGroupName`, `DuplicateLoadBalancerName`, `DuplicateListener`) is read back and used when it fits.
  - The order is every target group with its target, the load balancer, every listener. Nothing is rolled back when a step fails: running `ensure` again continues from there.
  - At the end the DNS name of the load balancer is read and resolved (retried every 5 seconds for a minute, since a new name does not resolve at once; failing that is a warning and exit code 0). The table is followed by a `# load balancer` block with the DNS name and one address per line, for scripts; `-o json` adds `"load_balancer": {"name", "arn", "type", "scheme", "state", "vpc_id", "dns_name", "ips", "listeners": [{"port", "protocol", "target_group"}]}`. `--wait-healthy` also waits until the instance is healthy in every target group.
  - The client address reaches the instance unchanged, so the instance's security groups must let the clients in on the listener ports (`aws ec2 security-group ensure ... --rule tcp:80:0.0.0.0/0`); a note on stderr says so. TLS on `tcp:443` passes through to the instance, which holds the certificate.
  - `--type application` and listener protocols other than `tcp` are exit code 7: build Application Load Balancers with `create`, `target-group` and `listener`.
  - `--dry-run` reads everything and reports what would be created or modified; a new load balancer's id is `(pending)` and no DNS name is printed (an existing load balancer's is). Report rows: `target-group/<name>`, `target-group/<name>/target` (detail `i-...:80`), `load-balancer/<name>`, `load-balancer/<name>/security-groups`, `listener/<lb>:<port>`.
- `target-group list` lists the groups of the region, or of one load balancer with `--lb`; `--vpc` filters. `get` shows the group, its health check and its targets with their health. JSON: `{"arn", "name", "protocol", "port", "vpc_id", "target_type", "load_balancers", "health_check": {"enabled", "protocol", "port", "path", "interval_seconds", "timeout_seconds", "healthy_threshold", "unhealthy_threshold"}, "region", "profile", "targets"}`.
- `target-group create <name>` needs `--protocol` and `--port`; the VPC is `--vpc` or `defaults.aws.vpc_id` (neither is exit code 2). The health check keeps the AWS defaults (30 seconds, a 10 second timeout) unless `--health-check-protocol`, `--health-check-port` or `--health-check-interval` (5 to 300 seconds, above the timeout) are given. `--target <id>[:<port>]` registers targets: instance ids for `instance` groups, IP addresses for `ip` groups (`[2001:db8::1]:80` for IPv6 with a port); a missing port is the group's. A group of that name is exit code 1.
- `target-group update` registers (`--add-target`), deregisters (`--remove-target`) or sets (`--set-target`: the targets of each port it names become exactly those given, the others on those ports are deregistered, other ports are left alone; it cannot be combined with the other two) targets, comparing id and port and ignoring draining ones; registered or absent ones are `unchanged`. A deregistered target drains first (300 seconds by default) and shows as `draining`. `--health-check-protocol` and `--health-check-interval` change the health check. Protocol, port, VPC and target type cannot change.
- `target-group delete` shows the group, its load balancers and targets and asks for a confirmation (as for `load-balancer delete`). A group a listener or rule forwards to is exit code 1 (`ResourceInUse`).
- `listener create <lb>` needs `--protocol`, `--port` and `--target-group` (in the load balancer's region). `TLS` and `HTTPS` need `--cert` (an ACM certificate ARN); other protocols refuse it (exit code 2). A listener on the port is exit code 1 (use `update`). `listener update` changes the target group and the protocol in place (switching to `TLS` or `HTTPS` without a certificate on the listener is exit code 2); values already set are `unchanged`. `listener delete` asks for a confirmation (as above); the target group stays.
- Errors: a missing load balancer, target group or listener is exit code 4; parameter errors AWS reports as `ValidationError` are exit code 2, except for target registration, where they mean an id that is no instance of the VPC, and the IP address type, where they depend on the subnets (exit code 1); denied calls are exit code 3 and name the IAM action (`elasticloadbalancing:CreateLoadBalancer`, ...; creating the first load balancer of an account also needs `iam:CreateServiceLinkedRole`); a resource in use, deletion protection, `InvalidConfigurationRequest` and quotas (`TooManyLoadBalancers`, `TooManyTargets`, ...) are exit code 1 with the AWS message; a wait that runs out of time is exit code 1 with the last state.

AWS Certificate Manager (`acm`) holds public certificates. ACM is a regional service: a certificate belongs to one region of one account (CloudFront uses those of `us-east-1`, a load balancer those of its own region). The inventory (`certificate`) holds the certificates of every enabled region and of every key type the SDK knows, with their primary name, state, type, key algorithm and expiry.

- Key algorithms: ACM lists only RSA certificates unless the key types are named, so `list` and `get` name them: `--key-type` (repeatable or comma-separated), by default `EC_prime256v1` and `RSA_2048` (`defaults.aws.acm.default_key_types`). The accepted values are `RSA_1024`, `RSA_2048`, `RSA_3072`, `RSA_4096`, `EC_prime256v1`, `EC_secp384r1`, `EC_secp521r1`, `ML_DSA_44`, `ML_DSA_65` and `ML_DSA_87` (any case, `-` or `_`; anything else is exit code 2). ACM answers with hyphens (`EC-prime256v1`); polyctl reports the underscore spelling everywhere.
- `certificate list` shows `DOMAIN`, `STATUS`, `TYPE`, `KEY`, `EXPIRES`, `REGION` and `ARN` of the profile's region from the inventory; `--region <r>` asks AWS about another region, `--region all` lists every region of the inventory. `--status` (`ISSUED`, `PENDING_VALIDATION`, `EXPIRED`, ...) and `--domain` (the primary name is the domain or its wildcard) filter. Several certificates of one domain are all listed. JSON: `[{"arn", "domain", "status", "type", "key_algorithm", "in_use", "not_after", "created_at", "profile", "region"}]` (`not_after` is `null` while the certificate is not issued); none is `[]`, a note on stderr and exit code 0.
- `certificate get <arn>` reads the certificate in the region of the ARN (another `--region` is exit code 2; a malformed ARN too, before anything is sent) with the profile of the ARN's account (`--profile`, the only AWS account, the account id in the profile's metadata or inventory, or the credentials asked live). `get <domain>` finds the certificates whose primary name is the domain or its wildcard in the inventory of every selected account (synced once when it does not know the domain); they must all be in one account and region (several are exit code 5: pass `--profile` and/or `--region`; `--region` asks that region of the profile's account directly). Of several, the best is read live: `ISSUED` before `PENDING_VALIDATION`, then the later expiry, then the newer one; expired, failed, timed out and revoked ones come last. Other names of a certificate are not matched.
