JSON: `{"arn", "domain", "sans", "status", "type", "key_algorithm", "issuer", "created_at", "issued_at", "not_before", "not_after", "renewal_eligibility", "in_use_by", "failure_reason", "validation_records", "profile", "region", "cached"}`; empty values are `null` or left out, `-` in the table. `--validation-records`, and every `PENDING_VALIDATION` certificate, add `validation_records: [{"domain", "validation_status", "name", "type", "value"}]` (one per distinct record, name and value as AWS writes them, with the trailing dot) and a `# validation records` block after the table. Imported certificates have none. A `PENDING_VALIDATION` certificate also gets a note on stderr with the hours left of ACM's 72-hour validation window and the `dns ensure` command that writes its records. A missing certificate is exit code 4 (and leaves the inventory); with `--offline`, so is an inventory that never held the certificates.
- `certificate request <domain>` requests a public certificate validated through DNS. By default (`--wildcard`, `defaults.aws.acm.wildcard`) it is `*.<domain>` with `<domain>` as a second name; `--wildcard=false` is `<domain>` alone, and a `<domain>` given as `*.example.com` means the wildcard. `--san` adds names (10 names per certificate unless the account's quota was raised: `LimitExceededException` is exit code 1 with a pointer to Service Quotas). Names are checked first (letters, digits and hyphens, a single leading `*.`; no scheme, path or space) and a `--san` that repeats a name of the certificate is exit code 2. The key algorithm is `--key-algorithm` (`defaults.aws.acm.default_key_algorithm`, built-in `EC_prime256v1`) and is always sent, since ACM picks `RSA_2048` without it. The tags of `defaults.aws.tags` and `--tag` go on the certificate. The account is `--profile` (or the current profile), the region `--region` (or the profile's).
  - A certificate of the same primary name, names (compared without case and order) and key algorithm that is `PENDING_VALIDATION` or `ISSUED` and issued by Amazon is reused: `unchanged`, with its records, and nothing is requested; a second run never makes a duplicate. A certificate of the name with other names or another key algorithm does not count (a note on stderr names it). The request also carries an idempotency token derived from the profile, region, names and key algorithm, so a retried request returns the same certificate.
  - ACM publishes the validation records some seconds after the request: `request` waits for them (2 second polls, at most `--timeout`, default 5m; `--no-wait` reads the certificate once and may find none). A certificate that leaves `PENDING_VALIDATION` without records (ACM refuses some names, such as those under `example.com`: `FAILED`, `ADDITIONAL_VERIFICATION_REQUIRED`) ends the wait at once with its failure reason (exit code 1). A wait that fails keeps the certificate: the report shows it, and the error says to read the records later with `get <arn> --validation-records`.
  - The report has `certificate/<domain>` (`created` or `unchanged`, the ARN in the detail) and one `certificate/<domain>/validation` row per distinct record (the wildcard and the bare name share theirs). JSON adds `"arn"` and `"validation_records"`. stderr prints the `polyctl dns ensure <domain> --record <name>:CNAME:<value>:300:false` command that writes them (full names, so they land right whichever zone serves `<domain>`; trailing dots removed, case kept, not proxied: a proxied CNAME on Cloudflare hides the target from ACM), with a command of its own for each `--san` outside `<domain>`. `request` never writes DNS itself, and does not wait for the issuance, which follows once ACM sees the records; keep the records while the certificate is in use, since ACM renews it through them.
  - `--dry-run` lists and reads as usual, then reports `would create` with the names, key algorithm and `DNS`, and `certificate/<domain>/validation  skipped  validation record available after request (dry-run)`; the JSON `arn` is `(pending)` and `validation_records` is `[]`. An existing equivalent certificate is `unchanged`, as for real.
- `certificate import <domain>` imports the certificate of the root domain of `<domain>` in the local store (`cert ensure`; `--staging` reads the staging store), or the PEM files of `--cert-file` (the leaf, or the leaf followed by its chain), `--chain-file` and `--key-file` (an unencrypted private key that belongs to the leaf; the leaf must cover `<domain>`). The leaf and the chain are sent apart, as ACM requires. An imported certificate (type `IMPORTED`, not expired) whose primary name is the domain or its wildcard in the region is re-imported in place: **its ARN stays**, so the load balancers and CloudFront distributions using it need no change, and its tags stay. The same serial number is `unchanged`; several such certificates are exit code 5 (pass `--arn`, or `--new`). `--arn` names the certificate to re-import into (its region is used). Without one, a new certificate is imported and tagged `polyctl:domain=<domain>`, with the tags of `defaults.aws.tags` and `--tag`. ACM cannot change the key algorithm of a certificate: a key of another type is exit code 1 before anything is sent, with `--new` (a new ARN, to be set where the old one is used) as the way out. An expired certificate is exit code 1 (ACM imports a certificate only during its validity period), an Amazon-issued one cannot be replaced (exit code 1). After the import the certificate is read back until it shows the new serial number. Report row: `certificate/<domain>` (`created`, `updated` or `unchanged`); JSON adds `"arn"`. `--dry-run` reports `would create` (`arn` `(pending)`) or `would update`.
- Errors: denied calls are exit code 3 and name the IAM action (`acm:RequestCertificate`, `acm:DescribeCertificate`, `acm:ListCertificates`, `acm:ImportCertificate`); other ACM errors (`ValidationException`, `InvalidDomainValidationOptionsException`, `InvalidParameterException`, ...) are exit code 1 with the AWS message. EMAIL validation is not offered.

RDS database instances (`rds instance`). Instances are created with MySQL only for now (`--engine` takes `mysql`; any other engine is exit code 7); the other commands work for any engine except Aurora, whose members are started, stopped and resized through their cluster (exit code 7). The inventory (`db_instance`) holds the instances of every enabled region, keyed by region and identifier (the same identifier in two regions is two instances).

- Every command but `list` and `create` takes the instance's identifier or its endpoint DNS name (an identifier holds no dot, so a name with a dot is an endpoint; compared without case and trailing dot) and finds the account itself: the inventory tells which account and region hold it; when it does not know it, the instances of every selected account are synced once and the lookup repeated. Several matches (the same identifier in two accounts or regions) are exit code 5: pass `--profile` and/or `--region`. Nothing found is exit code 4. An inventory row whose instance is gone is removed, the account synced again and the lookup repeated once. `--region <r>` with an identifier asks the selected profile's account in that region directly.
- `list` shows `ID`, `PROFILE`, `REGION`, `CLASS`, `STATUS`, `ENGINE`, `VERSION`, `STORAGE` and `ENDPOINT` (`host:port`) of the profile's region from the inventory; `--region <r>` shows another region, `--region all` every region; `--refresh` reads AWS again (`rds:DescribeDBInstances`, 100 per page), `--offline` only the inventory (an inventory that never held the instances is exit code 6). `--state` (the RDS status as it is: `available`, `stopped`, `creating`, ...), `--class` and `--name` (a glob pattern on the identifier) filter. JSON: `[{"id", "profile", "region", "class", "status", "engine", "engine_version", "allocated_storage_gb", "storage_type", "az", "multi_az", "endpoint", "port", "deletion_protection", "subnet_group", "security_groups", "created_at"}]` (`endpoint` is empty while the instance is created, `created_at` is RFC 3339 or empty). None is `[]`, a note on stderr and exit code 0.
- `get` reads the instance live (with `--offline`, the inventory's copy) and adds `parameter_group`, `backup_retention`, `publicly_accessible` and `tags` to the fields of `list`.
- `create <id>` creates a MySQL instance in the account of `--profile` (or the current profile) and the region of `--region` (or the profile's); it never walks the accounts. `--size` (GB) and `--az` are required (put the instance in the availability zone of the instances that use it: traffic across zones costs money). The master password comes from `--password-stdin` (one line of a pipe; asked without echo on a terminal) or `--password-env <VAR>`, never from an argument, and is at least 8 characters; it is never printed, logged or stored. The identifier must start with a letter and hold ASCII letters, digits and hyphens (no trailing or double hyphen, at most 63 characters); RDS keeps it in lower case.
  - The version is `--engine-version`, sent as it is, or with `latest` (the default) the newest available version of `--major` (`X.Y`, default `8.0` or `defaults.aws.rds.default_major`; without a major version `latest` would pick an innovation release such as 9.x). Versions are compared number by number (8.0.36 is newer than 8.0.9); a plain version wins over an `-rds.<date>` build of the same version, and among builds the latest date wins; deprecated versions are skipped. When asking for the major version answers nothing, the full list is filtered by prefix. No available version is exit code 1. The version used is printed on stderr.
  - The rest: `--class` (`defaults.aws.rds.default_class`, built-in `db.m5.large`), `--master-user admin`, `--storage-type` (`gp2`, `gp3`, `io1`, `io2` or `standard`; `defaults.aws.rds.default_storage_type`, built-in `gp3`; below the smallest size seen accepted, 5 GB for gp2 and standard, 20 GB for gp3, 100 GB for io1 and io2, is exit code 2), `--max-size` (storage autoscaling limit, 1000 GB; it must be above `--size`, and 0 turns autoscaling off), `--subnet-group`, `--security-group` and `--parameter-group` (`defaults.aws.rds.subnet_group`, `security_group_ids` and `parameter_group`; without any of them AWS uses the default VPC with its default subnet group and security group, and a note says so), `--backup-retention 7` days, `--backup-window 03:00-04:00` and `--maintenance-window sun:04:00-sun:05:00` (UTC), `--public=false`. Tags: `Name=<id>`, then `defaults.aws.tags`, `defaults.aws.rds.default_tags` and `--tag k=v`, a later one replacing a key.
  - An instance of that identifier in the region is exit code 1, also in a dry run. `create` waits until the instance is available (30 second polls, `--timeout 30m`; about 7 minutes for a small instance, longer for bigger ones), keeps waiting while a new instance is not visible yet, and ends at once on a status it does not come back from (`failed`, `incompatible-*`, `storage-full`, ...). A wait that fails or runs out of time keeps the instance (exit code 1 with the last status). The result is `RDS_ENDPOINT=<host>` and `RDS_PORT=<port>` lines, or `{"id", "endpoint", "port", "engine_version", "status"}` in JSON; `--wait=false` returns at once without the endpoint (`RDS_ID` and `RDS_STATUS` lines).
  - Errors keep the AWS code and message, with a hint: `InvalidVPCNetworkStateFault` reads the subnet group and says whether the zone is missing from it (listing its zones), whether the class is not offered in the zone with that storage, or that AWS may be short of capacity; `InsufficientDBInstanceCapacity` suggests another zone or class; `InvalidParameterValue` and `InvalidParameterCombination` say to check the values. They are exit code 1.
  - `--dry-run` resolves the version and checks the identifier, then reports `db-instance/<id>  would create  mysql <version> <class> <size>GB <type> az=<az>` (attrs `endpoint` `(pending)`) and `db-instance/<id>/wait  skipped  (dry-run)`.
- `start` and `stop` act on an instance that is `stopped` or `available`. One already in the target status is `unchanged` and nothing is sent, as is one on its way there (`starting` / `stopping`; `--wait` waits until it arrives). Any other status is exit code 1 (RDS itself refuses them with `InvalidDBInstanceState`). `stop` takes no snapshot. AWS refuses to stop read replicas, instances with read replicas and SQL Server Multi-AZ instances: the AWS error is the answer (exit code 1). `--wait` waits for `available` / `stopped` (15 second polls, `--timeout 30m`; about 4 minutes). Report row: `db-instance/<id>` with `stopped (was available)`, or the status AWS answered (`stopping (was available)`) without `--wait`. `--dry-run` reports `would update  available -> stopping`.
- **AWS starts a stopped instance again after 7 days.** `ensure <id>... --state stopped` keeps instances stopped when it runs every day, for example from cron: `0 3 * * * polyctl aws rds instance ensure db-test-01 db-test-02 --state stopped >>/var/log/polyctl-rds.log 2>&1`. Available instances are stopped; `stopped` and `stopping` ones are `unchanged`; one that is `starting`, `modifying`, `backing-up`, `configuring-*` or the like (an instance AWS just started again is `starting` for a few minutes) is waited for until it is available (30 second polls), then stopped; one that reaches `stopped` meanwhile is `unchanged`. Instances being created or deleted, failed, `incompatible-*`, `storage-full` or without access to their encryption key are `skipped` (the detail is the status) and count as failures. `--state running` (alias `available`) is the mirror image: stopped instances are started, `stopping` ones waited for and then started, available ones and those in a transitional status of a running instance are `unchanged`. ensure waits until each instance is in the state (`--no-wait` returns once the request is sent; `--timeout 30m` bounds each wait). Every instance is handled even when one fails (`--continue-on-error=false` stops at the first failure); the report lists all of them and the error `the following N instance(s) failed: a, b`. Exit code: 0 when all are as asked (unchanged included), 4 when every failure is a missing instance, 1 otherwise; a failure is sent to `hooks.on_failure` as `aws.rds.instance.ensure.failed`. `--dry-run` reports `would update` (`wait for available, then stop (now starting)` for a transitional one) and `unchanged` without waiting.
- `resize <id> --class <c>` changes the class at once (`ApplyImmediately`): **the instance restarts** (a few minutes of downtime; a failover for Multi-AZ), as a line on stderr says. The same class is `unchanged` and nothing is sent. An instance that is not available is waited for first (`--timeout`); a stopped one is exit code 1 (start it first), and one that disappears meanwhile exit code 4. resize waits until the new class is in place, nothing is pending and the instance is available again (15 second polls, `--timeout 60m`; about 5 minutes for a small instance; `--no-wait` returns at once), so the instance that still reads available with the old class right after the request does not end the wait early, and prints how long it took. AWS checks that the class fits the engine, the storage and the zone (exit code 1 with its message). `--dry-run` reports `would update  <old> -> <new> (apply immediately)`.
- `delete <id>` takes a final snapshot unless `--skip-final-snapshot`: `<id>-final-snapshot`, or `--final-snapshot <name>` (giving both is exit code 2). A snapshot of that name that exists already is exit code 1 before anything changes (pass another name or delete the old snapshot). Automated backups are deleted with the instance (the AWS default). The instance, its status, its deletion protection and the snapshot (or a warning that none is taken) are shown on stderr, then `Delete RDS instance <id>? (y/N)` is asked (default no; `--yes` skips it; the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2 without; answering no exits with code 0). With deletion protection on, it is switched off first (and read back), unless `--keep-protection`, which leaves it and is exit code 1; when the delete then fails, the error says that the protection was switched off. An instance being deleted is `unchanged`. `--wait` waits until it is gone (30 second polls, `--timeout 60m`; about 8 minutes). Report rows: `db-instance/<id>  updated  disable deletion protection` (when on), `db-instance/<id>  deleted  deleting; final snapshot <name>` and `db-instance/<id>/final-snapshot  created  <name>`. `DBSnapshotAlreadyExists` and `SnapshotQuotaExceeded` are exit code 1 with a hint. `--dry-run` asks nothing and reports the same rows as `would update`, `would delete` and `would create`. The instance leaves the inventory.
- Errors: denied calls are exit code 3 and name the IAM action (`rds:CreateDBInstance`, `rds:StopDBInstance`, `rds:DescribeDBInstances`, ...); an instance that is gone (`DBInstanceNotFound`) is exit code 4; other RDS errors, parameter errors included, are exit code 1 with the AWS code and message.

Route 53 stores a *record set* (name + type) with several values. polyctl exposes **one record per value** both in the inventory and in `dns list`, with a synthetic id of the form `<zone-id>|<TYPE>|<name>|<set-identifier>|<value>` (each part URL-encoded); updating or deleting one of them rewrites the whole record set with that value changed or removed. `record upsert` with several `--value` flags writes the set in one call.

### GCP (`polyctl gcp`)

```text
gcp whoami                        # the profile's project, account and login method
gcp project list                  gcp project current
gcp dns zone list                 gcp dns zone get <managed-zone>
gcp dns record list <zone> [--type] [--name]
gcp dns record upsert <zone> --type A --name www --value 203.0.113.10 [--value ...] [--ttl 300]
gcp dns record delete <zone> (--type A --name www [--value <v>] [--priority] | --id <zone|TYPE|name|value>)
gcp compute instance list                         gcp compute instance start <name|id> [--zone <z>] [--wait [--timeout 10m]]     gcp compute instance stop <name|id> [--zone <z>] [--yes] [--wait [--timeout 10m]]
gcp compute instance get <name|id> [--zone <z>] [--field external-ip|internal-ip|status|zone|machine-type]
gcp compute instance create <name> [--machine-type <type|size>] [--zone <z>] [--disk <GB>] [--boot-disk-size <GB>] [--disk-type pd-balanced] [--data-disk-type <t>]
                            [--os debian] [--os-version <v>] [--image <path>] [--ssh-key <key>]... [--ssh-key-file <path>]... [--ssh-user <name>]
                            [--ssh-key-instance-only] [--tag <tag>]... [--network <n>] [--subnet <s>] [--spot] [--ip-forward[=false]]
                            [--static-ip[=false]] [--service-account <email>] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m]
                            [--windows-user polyctl-admin] [--password-timeout 10m]                      # Windows only
gcp compute instance password <name|id> [--zone <z>] [--reset [--user <name>] [--password-timeout 10m] [--yes]]   # Windows user name and password
gcp compute instance delete <name|id> [--zone <z>] [--release-ips] [--yes] [--wait [--timeout 10m]]   # alias: terminate
gcp compute instance resize <name|id> --machine-type <type|size> [--zone <z>] [--start[=false]] [--yes] [--wait-port <n>] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute instance reboot <name|id|ip> [--zone <z>] [--yes] [--wait [--timeout 3m]]
gcp compute instance update <name|id> --ip-forward[=false] [--zone <z>]
gcp compute firewall list [--network <n>]          gcp compute firewall get <name>
gcp compute firewall create <name> --allow <proto[:ports]>... [--network <n>] [--source-range <cidr>]... [--destination-range <cidr>]...
                            [--target-tag <tag>]... [--direction ingress|egress] [--priority 1000] [--description <text>] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute firewall update <name> [--allow ...] [--source-range ... | --add-source-range <cidr>...] [--destination-range ...]
                            [--target-tag ...] [--description ...] [--priority ...] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute firewall delete <name> [--yes] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute firewall ensure [<name>] --preset lb-health-check [--network <n>] [--region <r>] [--instance <i> [--zone <z>]] [--target-tag <tag>]
                            [--allow ...] [--description ...] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute firewall ensure <name> --allow ... --source-range ... [--target-tag ...] [--network <n>] [--direction ...] [--priority ...]
gcp compute network list                          gcp compute network get <name>
gcp compute subnet list [--network <n>] [--region <r>] [--proxy-only]
gcp compute ssl-certificate list [--global | --region <r>] [--expiring-in <days>]
gcp compute ssl-certificate create <name> --cert-file <pem> --key-file <pem> (--global | --region <r>) [--description <text>]
gcp compute ssl-certificate update <name> --cert-file <pem> --key-file <pem> (--global | --region <r>) [--keep-old]
gcp compute ssl-certificate delete <name> (--global | --region <r>) [--yes]
gcp compute load-balancer cert set <lb> --domain <d> [--cert-file <pem> --key-file <pem>] [--days 30] [--new-ip] [--keep-old]
                            [--region <r> | --global] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute load-balancer ensure <instance> --protocol http|https|tcp [--port <n>] [--backend-port <n>] [--name <lb-name>]
                            [--health-check-port <n>] [--health-check-name <name>] [--ssl-certificate <name>] [--backend-protocol http|https]
                            [--address <name>] [--zone <z>] [--region <r>] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m] [--health-timeout 3m]
gcp compute load-balancer list [--global | --region <r>]
gcp compute load-balancer get <name> [--global | --region <r>]
gcp compute load-balancer delete <name> [--global | --region <r>] [--yes] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute backend-service list|get|delete [<name>] [--global | --region <r>] [--yes] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute forwarding-rule list|get|delete [<name>] [--global | --region <r>] [--yes] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute health-check list|get|delete [<name>] [--global | --region <r>] [--yes] [--timeout 10m]
gcp compute instance-group list|get|delete [<name>] [--zone <z>] [--yes] [--timeout 10m]
```

Inventory types: `zone`, `dns_record`, `instance`, `load_balancer` (forwarding rules, each with the name of its load balancer), `eip` (static addresses), `certificate` (SSL certificates). Cloud DNS record sets are split one record per value exactly like Route 53 (see above); writes are applied as a single atomic change.

Compute Engine instances:

- `get`, `start`, `stop`, `delete`, `resize`, `reboot` and `update` take the instance's name or numeric id and find the account themselves: the inventory of every selected GCP account (instances in every status, `TERMINATED` included) tells which project and zone hold it, and when none does the instance lists are synced once (always with `--refresh`). The instance is then read live, so these commands need the network (`--offline` is exit code 6). An instance of one name in several zones or projects is ambiguous (exit code 5): pass `--zone`, `--profile` or the id. `--zone` also finds an instance the inventory does not know when the sync failed; a `--zone` that differs from the zone of the only match is noted on stderr and the real zone is used. Nothing found is exit code 4. The inventory rows hold the internal and external IP and the network tags of each instance.
- `get` shows the instance with its addresses, network, tags, ip forwarding, deletion protection and disks (their type is read from each disk). In JSON: `{"name", "id", "zone", "status", "machine_type", "internal_ip", "external_ip", "network", "subnetwork", "tags", "can_ip_forward", "deletion_protection", "disks": [{"name", "boot", "size_gb", "type", "auto_delete"}], "creation_timestamp", "profile", "project"}`. `--field` prints one raw value for scripts (`external-ip`, `internal-ip`, `status`, `zone`, `machine-type`). An instance without an external IP gets a warning with the reason (a stopped instance released its ephemeral IP; a running one has no external access configured); with `--field external-ip` it is exit code 1 with nothing on stdout. A static external address stays bound to a stopped instance, which then still reports it.
- `start` and `stop`: an instance already `RUNNING` (`start`) or `TERMINATED` (`stop`) is `unchanged` and nothing is sent. `stop` asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`: exit code 2, before any lookup; a declined confirmation is exit code 0); a stopped instance loses its ephemeral external IP. `--wait` waits for the operation (`--timeout`, 10m). The output is a change report: `instance/<name>  updated  start; RUNNING` (with `--wait`; the IPs are in the JSON `attrs`), or `stop` / `start` without it. An instance that no selected account holds is exit code 4.
- `create <name>` creates the instance in `--zone` of the selected profile's project; it is not an ensure command: an instance of that name in the zone is exit code 1. The name must follow the Compute Engine rule (a lower-case letter first, then lower-case letters, digits and hyphens, at most 63 characters; a leading digit is exit code 2 with a hint to add a prefix). `--machine-type` and `--zone` default to `defaults.gcp.machine_type` and `defaults.gcp.zone`; neither is exit code 2. The machine type is checked in the zone first (exit code 2, as an unknown type on AWS); it may also be a size such as `2H4G` (see machine sizes below). The image is `--image` (`projects/<p>/global/images/<name>` or `.../family/<family>`; its minimum disk size is read when possible, 10 GB is assumed otherwise), or the latest current image of `--os` (`debian` by default, the system that boots from the smallest disk, 10 GB; `ubuntu`, `centos`, `rocky`, `almalinux`/`alma`, `rhel`, `windows`, `suse`, `opensuse`, `cos`) for the architecture of the machine type in the OS's public image project, every page listed and deprecated, obsolete, deleted, not `READY` and other-architecture images left out. The architecture is the one `MachineTypes.Get` reports for the machine type; when the API leaves it out, the Arm families (`t2a`, `c4a`, `n4a`, `a4x`) are arm64 and every other one x86_64, and a size always resolves to an x86_64 E2 type. An `--image` whose architecture is not the machine type's is exit code 2. No release number is written into polyctl: the highest one in the image list is picked on every run, so a new Debian release is used as soon as it is published. Debian takes the standard images `debian-<major>-<codename>[-arm64]-vYYYYMMDD` (no backports) and without `--os-version` picks the highest release number, then the newest image, Ubuntu the newest LTS (an even year with month 04; minimal images excluded), Rocky, AlmaLinux and RHEL their standard images (no optimized, EUS, LVM or SAP variants) of the highest major version, Windows the Server Datacenter image without Core (x86_64 only), and the others the newest image. No matching image is exit code 4, naming the architecture, the machine type, the zone and region, and `--image`; there is no fallback to another system. The boot disk `{name}-boot` (`--disk-type`, `pd-balanced` by default) has the image's minimum size unless `--boot-disk-size` (smaller than the minimum: exit code 2); `--disk <GB>` adds an empty data disk `{name}-data` (`--data-disk-type`). Both disks are deleted with the instance.
  - Network: `--network` (default `defaults.gcp.network`, else `default`); subnetwork: `--subnet` (default `defaults.gcp.subnet`; it must exist, exit code 4, and belong to the network, exit code 2), else the only subnetwork of the network in the zone's region (matched by exact network name; several are exit code 2, none is fine on an auto-mode network and exit code 1 on a custom-mode one).
  - `--static-ip` (default) reserves the external address `{name}-external-ip` (premium tier) and the internal address `{name}-internal-ip` (always in the instance's subnetwork) and binds them; existing ones are reused (`unchanged`), an internal one reserved in another subnetwork is re-reserved in the right one, and one that another resource uses stops create before anything is written (exit code 1). The names come from `naming.gcp_instance_external_ip` and `naming.gcp_instance_internal_ip`. `--static-ip=false` uses ephemeral addresses.
  - SSH keys go into the instance's `ssh-keys` metadata as `user:key` lines: `--ssh-key` takes the public key itself (a file path is refused with a hint to use `--ssh-key-file`), `--ssh-key-file` every key line of a file (`~` is expanded), and `defaults.gcp.ssh_keys` adds configured ones; both flags repeat and can be given together. This differs on purpose from `aws ec2 instance create` and `aliyun ecs instance create`, where an instance binds one key pair, so `--ssh-key` and `--ssh-key-file` take one key and exclude each other (exit code 2): Compute Engine metadata holds any number of keys. The user is a `user:` prefix of the key, else `--ssh-user`, else the name before the `@` of the e-mail address that ends the key's comment; none of them is exit code 2. Keys are parsed locally (Compute Engine does not check them). A key the project metadata already holds for that user is reported `ssh-key/<user> unchanged present in project metadata` and not repeated, unless `--ssh-key-instance-only`. A project whose metadata sets `block-project-ssh-keys=TRUE` (inherited by the instance) gets every key in the instance metadata. A project with `enable-oslogin=TRUE` gets a warning: OS Login ignores `ssh-keys`. Linux instances log in with an SSH key only: polyctl never sets or enables a password and writes no other metadata (no `windows-keys`, no startup script). A Linux instance (any image but `--os windows`, an image of the `windows-cloud` project or one with the `WINDOWS` guest OS feature) created without any key (no flag, no `defaults.gcp.ssh_keys`) writes nothing to the metadata and is accepted only when the project metadata holds SSH keys (and does not block them) or enables OS Login, with a note naming which; otherwise, and when the account may not read the project metadata, it is exit code 2 before anything is written (another failure to read it keeps its own exit code, 3 for an expired login).
  - `--tag` and `defaults.gcp.tags` are the network tags (both are used, without repeats). The instance gets the service account `--service-account` (default `default`, the default compute service account, with the devstorage read-only, logging, monitoring, service management, service control and trace scopes), Shielded VM with vTPM and integrity monitoring (secure boot off), on-host maintenance `MIGRATE` with automatic restart, or with `--spot` a Spot instance (`TERMINATE`, no automatic restart), no deletion protection, and ip forwarding only with `--ip-forward`.
  - A Windows instance gets a user and a password from its guest agent once it runs (`--windows-user`, `polyctl-admin` by default; `--password-timeout`, 10m), stored encrypted and shown as described in Instance login credentials; `gcp compute instance password <name|id>` shows them again, and `--reset` sets a new one.
  - By default create waits for the operation and then until the instance is `RUNNING` (5 second polls, `--timeout`, 10m by default, for each wait; running out of time is exit code 1) and prints its addresses; `--no-wait` returns once the creation was accepted (`instance/<name> created ... (provisioning)`). The report rows are `image/<os>`, `ssh-key/<user>`, `address/<name>`, `instance/<name>`, `instance/<name>/status` and, for Windows, `instance/<name>/windows-login`. Nothing is rolled back on failure: reserved addresses are kept, and the next create of the name reuses them. `--dry-run` runs every read (instance, machine type, image, subnetworks, addresses, project metadata) and reports `would create`, with `(pending)` for the IDs of addresses still to reserve.
- `delete` (alias `terminate`) shows the instance, its disks (which go with it, which stay) and the static addresses it keeps, and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2; a declined confirmation is exit code 0). An instance with deletion protection is exit code 1; polyctl never switches it off. `--release-ips` then deletes `{name}-external-ip` and `{name}-internal-ip` when they are `RESERVED` (Compute Engine itself would delete an address in use; polyctl skips one that another resource uses) and implies `--wait`. `--wait` waits until the instance is deleted (`--timeout`, 10m: deleting an instance with a data disk can take minutes). The instance leaves the inventory, and the Windows login polyctl stored for it is removed once the deletion is requested.
- **Machine sizes.** `--machine-type` of `create` and `resize` (and `defaults.gcp.machine_type`) also takes a size `<cores>H<memory>G`: `2H2G`, `2H4G`, `4H4G`, `6H12G` (any case). Anything else is a machine type, used as it is. A size is resolved in the zone on every run, in a `--dry-run` too, and never remembered. A predefined E2 type of exactly that shape comes first: `e2-micro` (2H1G), `e2-small` (2H2G), `e2-medium` (2H4G), `e2-standard-<n>` (n vCPUs, 4n GiB; 2 to 32), `e2-highcpu-<n>` (n vCPUs, n GiB; 2 to 32) and `e2-highmem-<n>` (n vCPUs, 8n GiB; 2 to 16); where two share a shape the shared-core type wins (`2H2G` is `e2-small`, not `e2-highcpu-2`). It is read in the zone (`machineTypes.get`); when the zone lacks it, or no predefined type has the shape, the size is the E2 custom type `e2-custom-<cores>-<MiB>` (`2H3G` is `e2-custom-2-3072`), which needs an even number of 2 to 32 vCPUs, 0.5 to 8 GiB of memory per vCPU, at most 128 GiB, in multiples of 0.25 GiB, and a zone that offers E2 types (`e2-standard-2` is read to tell). The choice is printed on stderr (`2H4G -> e2-medium (us-central1-a, E2 predefined)`) and the change report shows the type with the size (`e2-medium (2H4G)`, `e2-custom-2-3072 (2H3G, E2 custom)`). No fit is exit code 4, with the nearest E2 size (`1H1G`: `2H1G (e2-micro)`); a size of 0 vCPUs or 0 GiB is exit code 2. `resize` leaves an instance whose machine type already has the size `unchanged`.
- `resize --machine-type <t>` checks the type in the instance's zone first (exit code 2, nothing written: Compute Engine lists machine types per zone, so a type that exists elsewhere but not in this zone is exit code 2 as well); the same type is `unchanged`. A `RUNNING` instance is stopped after a confirmation (as for `delete`). Before that, its ephemeral IPs are made static, since a stopped instance loses its ephemeral external IP: an address that already holds the IP is `unchanged`, otherwise the IP is reserved as `{name}-external-ip` / `{name}-internal-ip`; a failed external promotion stops the command before the instance is stopped (exit code 1), a failed internal one is a warning. Then the instance is stopped (polled until `TERMINATED`), the type is changed and the instance started again (polled until `RUNNING`), each wait bounded by `--timeout`. The instance ends in the power state it had: a `TERMINATED` instance is changed and stays stopped unless `--start`, and `--start=false` leaves a `RUNNING` one stopped. A failed type change leaves the instance stopped with its old type. Report rows: `address/<name>`, `instance/<name>/stop`, `instance/<name>/machine-type` (`<old> -> <new>`), `instance/<name>/start`; an address that changed anyway is a warning `IP changed: <old> -> <new>`. `--wait-port <n>` then waits up to 5 minutes until that TCP port (22 for SSH) accepts connections on the internal IP, or the external one (exit code 1 otherwise); `--wait-ssh`, its old name for port 22, still works and is deprecated.
- `reboot` is a hard reset (`instances.reset`, like `gcloud compute instances reset`): the virtual machine restarts at once without a graceful shutdown of the operating system, keeps its addresses and stays `RUNNING`; it is never emulated by a stop and a start. It also takes an internal or external IP, looked up in the inventory; an IP that instances of several projects share is ambiguous (exit code 5), and the interactive flow lets you choose. The status is read live: an instance that is not `RUNNING` is exit code 1. The project, zone, addresses and status are shown and a confirmation is asked (as for `delete`). `--wait` waits for the reset (`--timeout`, 3m); a failed operation is exit code 1 with its error code.
- `update --ip-forward[=false]` switches ip forwarding in place, on a running or a stopped instance: the instance is sent back whole with its fingerprint and only `canIpForward` changed, allowing at most a refresh (never a restart), and read again to check the value. A value that is already set is `unchanged`; a stale fingerprint (the instance changed in between) is re-read and retried once. `update` without a setting is exit code 2.
- Errors: a disabled Compute Engine API and a missing permission (`compute.instances.*`, `compute.addresses.*`, ...) are exit code 3 and say which; quota and capacity errors (`QUOTA_EXCEEDED`, `ZONE_RESOURCE_POOL_EXHAUSTED`) are exit code 1 with a hint to try another zone or machine type, whatever HTTP status carries them; an operation that finishes with an error (`IP_IN_USE_BY_ANOTHER_RESOURCE`, ...) is exit code 1 with its code.

VPC firewall rules (read live, never kept in the inventory; `--profile` picks the project, and several GCP profiles without a default one are exit code 5). A rule name is unique in the project, so `get`, `update` and `delete` need no network:

- `list` lists the rules of every network, or of `--network` (matched by its exact name: `net-a` never lists the rules of `net-a-2`), sorted by priority (lower numbers win; at the same priority a deny rule wins over an allow rule). Columns `NAME NETWORK DIR PRI ALLOW SOURCES TARGET-TAGS` (`SOURCES` counts the source ranges, or the destination ranges of an egress rule; a deny rule shows `deny <protocols>`). No rule is exit code 0 with `[]` and a note.
- `get <name>`: JSON `{"name", "network", "direction", "priority", "allowed": [{"protocol", "ports"}], "denied", "source_ranges", "destination_ranges", "source_tags", "target_tags", "disabled", "description", "creation_timestamp"}` (`denied`, `destination_ranges` and `source_tags` only when set). A missing rule is exit code 4.
- `create <name>` is not an ensure: a rule of that name is exit code 1 (use `firewall ensure` to converge one). `--allow` (required, repeatable) is `<protocol>` or `<protocol>:<ports>`, the protocol `tcp`, `udp`, `icmp` or `all`, and only `tcp` and `udp` take ports: numbers, `from-to` ranges and the port aliases (`ssh`, `http`, `https`, `mysql`, ...), separated by commas (`tcp:80,443,8000-8100`); ports on another protocol are exit code 2. Without ports a protocol covers every port. An ingress rule (the default `--direction`) needs `--source-range`: Compute Engine silently opens a rule without one to `0.0.0.0/0`, so polyctl refuses it (exit code 2; pass `--source-range 0.0.0.0/0` to mean that). `--direction egress` takes `--destination-range` instead (required for the same reason) and refuses `--source-range`. CIDRs are normalized (a bare address gets `/32` or `/128`). Without `--target-tag` the rule applies to every instance of the network. `--network` defaults to `defaults.gcp.network`, else `default`; a missing network is exit code 4. `--priority` is 0-65535 (default 1000). The name must follow the Compute Engine rule (exit code 2).
- `update <name>` patches only the fields it is given: `--allow`, `--source-range`, `--destination-range` and `--target-tag` replace that list as a whole, `--add-source-range` adds ranges to the existing ones (a union; it never removes one), `--description` and `--priority` set those fields. Values already set are `unchanged` and nothing is sent; no field at all is exit code 2. The network and the direction are never changed (exit code 2: delete the rule and create it again), source ranges on an egress rule and destination ranges on an ingress rule are exit code 2, and a list cannot be emptied, except `--target-tag ""`, which makes the rule apply to every instance of the network: Compute Engine ignores an empty list in a patch, so the rule is then read and sent back whole (a warning is printed).
- `delete <name>` shows the rule and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2; a declined confirmation is exit code 0). A missing rule is exit code 4.
- `ensure` creates a missing rule, and adds to an existing one the ranges, target tags and allowed protocols or ports it lacks (a union: nothing is ever removed, so a range of a deleted subnetwork stays until `update --source-range` replaces the list); `--priority` and `--description` change an existing rule only when given. A rule without target tags already applies to every instance of the network, so it gets no tag (that would narrow it). A rule of that name on another network, with the other direction, disabled, a deny rule, or one that targets service accounts is exit code 1 with nothing written: polyctl does not move, turn, re-enable or retarget a rule. Without `--preset` it takes `<name>`, `--allow` and the ranges as `create` does.
  - `--preset lb-health-check` makes the rule load balancer health checks and proxies need. The name is `allow-lb-health-check-{network}` (`naming.gcp_lb_firewall`, which can use `{network}` and `{region}`; `<name>` overrides it; a name longer than 63 characters is cut and ends with a 6-character hash, the same for the same network). The source ranges are Google's health check ranges `35.191.0.0/16` and `130.211.0.0/22`, plus the primary and secondary ranges of every subnetwork of the network in `--region`, proxy-only subnetworks included (purpose `REGIONAL_MANAGED_PROXY`, `GLOBAL_MANAGED_PROXY`, `INTERNAL_HTTPS_LOAD_BALANCER`), sorted and without repeats. Subnetworks are regional: run it again for each region whose load balancers use the network, and the rule widens to those regions only. The target tag is `defaults.gcp.lb_network_tag` (`lb-health-check`) or `--target-tag`; the rule is an ingress rule at priority 1000 that allows every tcp port (an existing rule that allows tcp on some ports is kept as it is; `--allow` asks for other protocols or ports, which are then added as needed). `--source-range`, `--destination-range`, `--direction` and `--priority` are exit code 2 with the preset.
  - `--instance` (name or id, found like `instance get` finds it; `--zone` narrows it) also adds the target tag to that instance, in any status: the tags are replaced as a whole, so the instance's other tags are sent along with the fingerprint of the tags as read, and a stale fingerprint is read again and retried once. The account that holds the instance is used for the rule as well (a note on stderr when it is not the default profile), and the instance's network and region are the defaults of `--network` and `--region`; an instance that is not on `--network` is exit code 2. Without `--instance`, `--network` defaults to `defaults.gcp.network`, else `default`, and `--region` to the region of `defaults.gcp.zone`; no region at all is exit code 2.
  - Report rows: `firewall/<name>` (`created` with `tcp, 7 source ranges, tag lb-health-check`, `updated` with the added items such as `+10.20.0.0/24 (now 6 ranges)`, or `unchanged`) and `instance/<name>/tag`. In JSON the `attrs` of the rule carry `source_ranges`, the final list. `--dry-run` reads everything and reports `would create` / `would update`. A missing permission is exit code 3; failures also go to `hooks.on_failure` as `gcp.compute.firewall.ensure.failed`.
- Every write waits for its operation (`--timeout`, 10m; running out of time is exit code 1), since the operation is where Compute Engine reports most errors; `--no-wait` only submits it (writes to the same rule then fail until the operation is done). The writes print a change report (`firewall/<name>` `created`, `updated` with the changed fields, `deleted`); `--dry-run` runs the reads and reports `would create`, `would update` or `would delete`. A missing `compute.firewalls.*` permission and a disabled API are exit code 3 and say which; the firewall rule quota is exit code 1.

VPC networks and subnetworks (read-only, always read live, never kept in the inventory; `--profile` picks the project, and several GCP profiles without a default one are exit code 5):

- `network list` shows each network's mode (`auto`, `custom`, or `legacy` for a network with a single IPv4 range), routing mode, MTU (a network that never set one reads back 0 and is shown as `1460(default)`) and number of subnetworks. JSON: `[{"name", "mode", "routing_mode", "mtu", "ipv4_range", "subnetworks": ["<region>/<name>", ...], "description", "creation_timestamp"}]`.
- `network get <name>` adds the subnetworks of every region with their ranges (`subnets`, shaped like the rows of `subnet list`). A missing network is exit code 4.
- `subnet list` lists every region (`--region` one), optionally the subnetworks of one `--network`: the network is matched by its exact name, so `net-a` never lists the subnetworks of `net-a-2`. Columns `NAME REGION NETWORK RANGE SECONDARY-RANGES PURPOSE ROLE`; JSON `[{"name", "region", "network", "ip_cidr_range", "secondary_ranges": [{"range_name", "ip_cidr_range"}], "purpose", "role"}]`. `--proxy-only` keeps the proxy-only subnetworks (purpose `REGIONAL_MANAGED_PROXY`, `GLOBAL_MANAGED_PROXY` or the older `INTERNAL_HTTPS_LOAD_BALANCER`). No match is exit code 0 with `[]` and a note on stderr.
- A `--profile` that exists but holds no GCP account is exit code 7.

SSL certificates of load balancers (self-managed ones are uploaded by polyctl; Google-managed ones are shown and never replaced or renewed by polyctl). A regional certificate serves the regional load balancers of its region, a global one global load balancers; `create`, `update` and `delete` need `--region <r>` or `--global` (neither, or both, is exit code 2). Certificates are read live (`--profile` picks the project; several GCP profiles without a default one are exit code 5); `create` and `update` upsert them into the inventory type `certificate`, `delete` removes them, and `polyctl sync` lists them all.

- `list` reads every region and the global scope (`--region` or `--global` narrows it). Columns `NAME SCOPE REGION TYPE DOMAINS EXPIRES DAYS USED-BY`: the expiry in UTC (Compute Engine reports it with the offset of its own time zone), the whole days left (rounded down) and the target HTTPS proxies of every scope that use the certificate. A self-managed certificate no proxy uses is marked `*` after its name: an orphan, typically the old certificate of a replacement whose deletion failed, to delete by hand. `--expiring-in <days>` keeps the certificates with at most that many days left. JSON: `[{"name", "scope", "region", "type", "domains", "expire_time", "days_remaining", "description", "creation_timestamp", "used_by"}]` (`days_remaining` is `null` when the expiry is unknown, as for a managed certificate still being provisioned). No certificate is exit code 0 with `[]` and a note.
- `create <name>` uploads `--cert-file` (the PEM chain, leaf first: `fullchain.pem`) and `--key-file` (an unencrypted PEM key: PKCS#8, SEC 1 or PKCS#1); `@-` reads stdin, and both `@-` read one text holding the chain and the key. The files are checked before anything is sent, since Compute Engine accepts a key that does not belong to the certificate and an expired certificate without a word: unreadable PEM, an encrypted key, a key that does not match the leaf and an expired leaf are exit code 2. RSA and ECDSA keys of any size Compute Engine takes are accepted. The name must follow the Compute Engine rule (a lower-case letter first, then lower-case letters, digits and hyphens, at most 63 characters: `163-com-cert` and `example.com` are exit code 2). A certificate of that name in the scope is exit code 1. `--description` defaults to `SSL certificate for <domain>`.
- `update <name>` replaces a certificate. Certificates are immutable and cannot be deleted while a proxy uses them, so it creates a new certificate named after the domain of the new leaf and the day, `{slug}-cert-{YYYYMMDD}` (`naming.gcp_ssl_certificate`; the slug is the domain in lower case with dots as hyphens, `*.` as `wildcard-`, a `cert-` prefix when it would start with a digit, and a 6-character hash when the name would be too long; `-2`, `-3`... when the name already holds another certificate), switches every target HTTPS proxy of the scope that uses `<name>` to it (only `<name>` is swapped; the proxy's other certificates stay, and a proxy that would hold more than 15 certificates is exit code 1 before anything is written), and then deletes `<name>` unless `--keep-old`. Leaves are compared by the SHA-256 of their DER, never as PEM text: a certificate that already holds the new leaf is `unchanged`, and a certificate of the candidate name that holds it (a run whose switch failed) is reused instead of uploading it again. An old certificate still in use elsewhere is `skipped` (`still used by <proxy>`), not a failure. A missing certificate is exit code 4; a Google-managed one is exit code 1.
- `delete <name>` shows the certificate and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2; a declined confirmation is exit code 0). A certificate a proxy uses is exit code 1, naming the proxies; a missing one is exit code 4.
- `load-balancer cert set <lb> --domain <d>` puts the certificate of a domain on a load balancer. A load balancer has no certificate of its own: certificates hang on its target HTTPS proxies, so the command changes proxies and certificates only, never backend services or URL maps. `<lb>` is the name of a forwarding rule (looked up in the inventory, synced once when unknown) or of the load balancer, that is its URL map, looked up live in the selected GCP accounts; that tells the project and the scope (several matches are exit code 5: pass `--profile`, `--region` or `--global`; none is exit code 4; a `--profile` without a GCP account is exit code 7). It needs the network (`--offline` is exit code 6).
  - The certificate is `--cert-file` and `--key-file` (checked as for `create`; one that does not cover `--domain`, an exact name or a matching one-label wildcard, is exit code 2 before any request), or by default the certificate of the local store (`polyctl cert ensure`), issued or renewed first when it has `--days` whole days left or fewer (default `cert.renew_days`, 30); its `cert/<root>` row heads the report. A domain the store cannot issue (no configured DNS account holds it, a rate limit) is exit code 1 with a hint to pass the files. `--dry-run` never asks the CA for anything: a certificate that would be issued is reported `would create cert/<root> ... (not issued in dry-run)` and the following steps are still planned.
  - Every target HTTPS proxy whose URL map is the load balancer's (matched by the URL map's exact name, never by a part of the proxy name) is switched as by `update`: a new certificate `{slug}-cert-{YYYYMMDD}` of `--domain` in the load balancer's scope (or a certificate of that name that already holds the leaf), the certificates of the domain on the proxy replaced by it (a certificate's domain is its first DNS name that is not a wildcard; other domains stay), the replaced ones deleted after the switch unless `--keep-old` (one still in use elsewhere is `skipped`). A proxy that already holds the same leaf is `unchanged`, so a second run writes nothing; a run that created the certificate and failed to switch resumes with the switch; a run that switched and failed to delete leaves the old certificate, which `list` marks as an orphan.
  - A regional load balancer with only an HTTP proxy is upgraded: the certificate, an HTTPS proxy on the same URL map named after the HTTP proxy (`proxy-web-1` becomes `proxy-https-web-1`, after `naming.gcp_proxy_prefix`; a name without the prefix gets `-https`), and a `:443` forwarding rule named after the `:80` rule (`fr-web-1` becomes `fr-https-web-1`, after `naming.gcp_forwarding_rule_prefix`) with the network, tier and scheme of the `:80` rule and its labels (written after the rule exists, since Compute Engine ignores labels sent with the creation). The `:443` rule takes the IP of the `:80` rule so that DNS keeps working: an ephemeral IP is first reserved as the static address `<lb>-ip` (`address/<lb>-ip created promoted from ephemeral <ip>`); an IP that already is a static address is used as it is. `--new-ip` gives the `:443` rule a new ephemeral IP instead (DNS must then change). A regional load balancer whose HTTPS proxy has no `:443` rule gets one the same way (skipped when there is no `:80` rule to copy). Without any `:80` rule to copy, an upgrade is exit code 1; an HTTPS proxy or `:443` rule name that is taken by a resource of another URL map or proxy is exit code 1. A global load balancer without an HTTPS proxy is exit code 1 (only regional ones are upgraded; polyctl creates no global forwarding rules and only warns when a global HTTPS proxy has no `:443` rule). The URL of the new rule, `https://<ip>/`, is printed on stderr.
  - Report rows: `cert/<root>`, `ssl-certificate/<name>` (`created regional, <region>, expires <date>`), `target-https-proxy/<name>` (`updated certs: <old> -> <new>`, or `created`), `address/<name>`, `forwarding-rule/<name>` and the old `ssl-certificate/<name>` (`deleted`, `skipped`); in JSON (`{"target", "dry_run", "changes"}`) the `attrs` carry `scope`, `region`, `proxy`, `old` and `new`. Certificate and proxy operations are always waited for, since each step needs the previous one; `--no-wait` returns once a new `:443` rule was requested (its labels are then not copied). `--timeout` (10m) bounds each wait; running out of time is exit code 1.
- Writes wait for their operations (seconds) and print a change report (`ssl-certificate/<name>`, `target-https-proxy/<name>`); `--dry-run` runs the reads and reports `would create`, `would update`, `would delete`. A missing permission (`compute.sslCertificates.*`, `compute.targetHttpsProxies.setSslCertificates` or its regional form) and a disabled API are exit code 3.

Load balancers (regional external Application and proxy Network Load Balancers, `EXTERNAL_MANAGED`). A load balancer is no resource of its own in Compute Engine: it is a forwarding rule and the chain behind it (a target proxy, a URL map, backend services, health checks, instance groups). polyctl names a forwarding rule's load balancer after its `polyctl-lb` label, else the URL map of its target proxy (load balancers built by hand or by older tools, whatever their naming), else the rule's own name; the inventory (`load_balancer`) keeps that name with every forwarding rule.

- `ensure <instance> --protocol http|https|tcp` builds, or completes, a load balancer in front of an existing instance (name or id, found like `instance get` finds it, in any status; a `--zone`, `--region` or default profile that disagrees with the instance is noted and the instance's own values are used). It goes into the instance's region and network (the network of its first interface; unknown, `defaults.gcp.network`, else `default`, with a warning). Each component is read first, created when missing, completed in place where that is safe, and otherwise compared: a difference polyctl cannot change in place (a forwarding rule with another target, port, scheme or IP, a backend service with another protocol, port name, scheme or health check, a URL map or proxy pointing elsewhere, a health check of another type or port, a named port of that name on another port) is exit code 1 with nothing written, naming what to delete or suggesting `--name`. Nothing is ever deleted or rolled back: a failed run keeps what it built, and the next run reuses it and goes on from the failed step. A second run writes nothing.
  - Before anything is written: `--protocol https` needs `--ssl-certificate`, an existing regional certificate of the region (missing is exit code 4 with a hint to `ssl-certificate create`); the network needs an active proxy-only subnetwork in the region (purpose `REGIONAL_MANAGED_PROXY`, role `ACTIVE`; none is exit code 1 with an example `gcloud compute networks subnets create` command), since every forwarding rule would fail without one. Both checks run in a dry run too.
  - Components, in order: the health check firewall rule and the network tag of the instance, as `firewall ensure --preset lb-health-check --instance` makes them (a missing permission, as when a shared VPC host project manages the rules, is `skipped` with the reason and the ensure goes on; any other error stops it); the regional TCP health check `check-{port}` (`--health-check-name`; interval 10s, timeout 5s, server-default thresholds), shared by the load balancers of the region; the zonal instance group `ig-{instance}` with the instance as member and the named port of the backend, `http:80` for HTTP(S) to port 80, else `port-{port}:{port}` (named ports are replaced as a whole by Compute Engine, so the group's other named ports are always kept, with the fingerprint as read); the backend service `bs-{instance}` (tcp: `bs-tcp-{instance}`; protocol `HTTP`, or `HTTPS` with `--backend-protocol https`, or `TCP`; port name = the named port; utilization balancing at 0.8; timeout 30s), which gets the instance group added when it lacks it; for http and https the URL map `lb-{instance}` and the target HTTP proxy `proxy-{instance}` or target HTTPS proxy `proxy-https-{instance}` with the certificate (the certificates of an existing HTTPS proxy are the business of `load-balancer cert set` and stay); for tcp the target TCP proxy `proxy-tcp-{instance}`; then the forwarding rule `fr-{instance}` (`fr-https-`, `fr-tcp-`) on `--port` in the instance's network, premium tier, and its `polyctl-lb` label (written after the rule exists, since Compute Engine ignores labels sent with the creation; a failure there is a warning and `skipped`, and a rule labelled for another load balancer keeps its label).
  - An instance may belong to one instance group that a backend service uses: an instance already in such a group (matched by its exact name, never by a part of it) keeps it, and its new load balancer adds its named port there. So `--protocol http` and then `--protocol tcp --port 3306` share `ig-web-1` with the named ports `http:80` and `port-3306:3306`, and `--protocol http` then `--protocol https` share the URL map, backend service and instance group as well, each adding its proxy and forwarding rule.
  - Defaults: `--port` 80 (http), 443 (https), required for tcp (exit code 2); `--backend-port` 80 for http and https, `--port` for tcp; `--health-check-port` the backend port. `--name` names the load balancer (default `lb-{instance}`, tcp `lb-tcp-{instance}`) and the other components follow it (`--name lb-site` gives `ig-site`, `bs-site`, `proxy-site`, `fr-site`); the prefixes are `naming.gcp_lb_prefix`, `gcp_instance_group_prefix`, `gcp_backend_service_prefix`, `gcp_proxy_prefix` and `gcp_forwarding_rule_prefix`, so an instance name starting with a digit still gives valid names. A name longer than 63 characters is cut and ends with a 6-character hash, the same each time. An invalid `--name`, `--health-check-name`, `--ssl-certificate` or `--address` (upper case, `_`) is exit code 2. `--address <name>` puts the rule on a regional static external address, reserved first when missing (recommended name `{lb-name}-ip`, as `cert set` names the address it promotes); when the rule already exists, the missing address is reserved with the rule's current IP, which so becomes static (an existing address with another IP is exit code 1: a rule's IP cannot change in place). Without it the rule gets an ephemeral IP, which goes with the rule.
  - Every operation is waited for (`--timeout` bounds each, 10m; creating a backend service or a forwarding rule takes one to two minutes each), since each component needs the previous one. Then http and https wait up to `--health-timeout` (3m) for a `HEALTHY` backend, reading the backend health every 10 seconds: a backend that just joined reports no status at all for about two minutes, so no status and `UNHEALTHY` keep the wait going. Running out of time is `skipped` with the last state and a checklist on stderr (the port listens, the firewall rule, the network tag), exit code 0: the load balancer is built. `--no-wait` skips that wait; tcp never waits for health.
  - Report rows: `firewall/<name>`, `instance/<name>/tag`, `health-check/<name>`, `instance-group/<name>` (`created zone <z>, named-port http:80`, `updated +named-port port-3306:3306 (kept http:80)`), `instance-group/<name>/member`, `backend-service/<name>` (`HTTP, port-name http, EXTERNAL_MANAGED`, `updated +backend <group>`), `url-map/<name>`, `target-http-proxy/<name>` / `target-https-proxy/<name>` / `target-tcp-proxy/<name>`, `address/<name>`, `forwarding-rule/<name>` (`<ip>:<port>`), `forwarding-rule/<name>/labels` and `load-balancer/<name>/backend-health` (`HEALTHY`). JSON: `{"target": {"platform", "profile", "region", "zone", "project"}, "dry_run", "load_balancer": {"name", "protocol", "scope", "region", "ip_address", "port", "url"}, "changes"}`; the address (`http://<ip>/`, `https://<ip>/`, `<ip>:<port>`) also goes to stderr. A component created meanwhile by another run (409 `alreadyExists`) is read again and compared like an existing one. `--dry-run` runs every read and reports `would create` / `would update`, the IP of a new rule as `(pending)` and the health wait `skipped dry-run`. Failures also go to `hooks.on_failure` as `gcp.compute.load-balancer.ensure.failed`; the forwarding rule is upserted into the inventory.
- `list` reads the load balancers of the profile's project live, one row per forwarding rule: `NAME SCOPE REGION PROTOCOL IP PORT BACKEND HEALTH`, health being `HEALTHY`, `1/2 HEALTHY`, `UNHEALTHY`, `no status yet` or `-` when it cannot be read. Every scope by default; `--region` or `--global` narrows it. JSON: `[{"name", "scope", "region", "protocol", "ip_address", "port", "forwarding_rule", "target", "url_map", "backend_services", "health", "profile"}]`; none is `[]` with a note, exit code 0.
- `get <name>` shows the whole chain: forwarding rules, proxies (with their certificates), URL maps, backend services with the health of every instance, health checks and instance groups with their named ports and members. `<name>` is the load balancer name or the name of one of its forwarding rules; the inventory tells which accounts hold it (synced once when it is unknown, always with `--refresh`), then everything is read live (`--offline` is exit code 6). Without `--region` or `--global` a regional load balancer wins over a global one of the same name; one name in several projects or regions is exit code 5 (`--profile`, `--region`, `--global`); none is exit code 4. JSON: `{"name", "profile", "project", "scope", "region", "forwarding_rules", "proxies", "url_maps", "backend_services", "health_checks", "instance_groups"}`.
- `delete <name>` (found like `get`) deletes the forwarding rules of the load balancer, then the target proxies, URL maps and backend services that only those rules reach, in that order, waiting for each (`--timeout`, 10m); a component something else still uses is kept and reported `skipped used by ...`, one already gone `skipped already deleted`. Instance groups, health checks, firewall rules and static addresses are never deleted (other load balancers share them; delete them with their own commands). It shows the plan and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2; a declined confirmation is exit code 0); `--dry-run` reports `would delete`.
- The components on their own, for troubleshooting and clean-up: `backend-service`, `forwarding-rule` and `health-check` (regional and global; `--region` or `--global` narrows) and `instance-group` (zonal; `--zone` narrows), each with `list`, `get` and `delete`, read live. `list` reads the profile's project in every scope (columns `NAME SCOPE REGION PROTOCOL SCHEME PORT-NAME HEALTH-CHECKS BACKENDS`, `NAME SCOPE REGION IP PORT PROTOCOL SCHEME TARGET`, `NAME SCOPE REGION TYPE PORT INTERVAL TIMEOUT`, `NAME ZONE SIZE NAMED-PORTS NETWORK`; none is `[]` with a note, exit code 0). `get <name>` searches every selected GCP account; without a scope flag a regional match wins over a global one; several matches are exit code 5, none exit code 4. `backend-service get` adds the health of every instance behind each backend, `instance-group get` the members, `forwarding-rule get` its load balancer label. `delete <name>` shows the component and asks for a confirmation (default no; `--yes` skips it, the non-interactive flow needs `--yes`, exit code 2), waits for the operation (`--timeout`, 10m) and reports `deleted`; a component another one still uses (a health check of a backend service, an instance group of a backend service, a backend service of a URL map or proxy, a proxy of a forwarding rule) is exit code 1 naming the user, since Compute Engine refuses it; `--dry-run` reports `would delete`.

### Alibaba Cloud (`polyctl aliyun`)

```text
aliyun whoami                                     # sts GetCallerIdentity: account, ARN, identity type
aliyun dns domain list                            # Alidns domains (zones)
aliyun dns record list <domain> [--type] [--name]
aliyun dns record create <domain> --type A --name www --value 203.0.113.10 [--ttl 600] [--priority] [--line default|telecom|unicom|mobile|oversea]
aliyun dns record update <domain> <record-id> [--name] [--type] [--value] [--ttl] [--priority] [--line]
aliyun dns record delete <domain> <record-id>
aliyun domain list                                aliyun domain get <domain>       # registered domains
aliyun domain ns get <domain>                     aliyun domain ns set <domain> <nameserver>...
aliyun ecs instance list [--region]

aliyun ecs instance create <name> --type <type|size> --security-group <name|id> [--vswitch <id|name>] [--image <id> | --os alibaba-cloud-linux|windows] [--disk 20] [--disk-category cloud_essd_entry] [--key-pair <name>] [--ssh-key <public key> | --ssh-key-file <path>] [--eip] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m] [--region <r>]

[--key-pair <name>] [--ssh-key <public key> | --ssh-key-file <path>] [--eip[=false]] [--no-wait] [--timeout 10m] [--region <r>]

aliyun ecs image latest [--os alibaba-cloud-linux|windows] [--arch x86_64|arm64] [--region <r>]

aliyun eip address list [--unused | --bound] [--region <r>]

aliyun alb server-group list [--name <name>] [--region <r>]
